Existence and stability of infinite time blow-up in the Keller-Segel system
Juan Dávila, Manuel del Pino, Jean Dolbeault, Monica Musso, Juncheng Wei

Abstract
. Perhaps the most classical diffusion model for chemotaxis is the Keller-Segel system ( ∗ \ast ) { u t = Δ u − ∇ ⋅ ( u ∇ v ) in ℝ 2 × ( 0 , ∞ ) , v = ( − Δ ℝ 2 ) − 1 ​ u := 1 2 ​ π ​ ∫ ℝ 2 log ⁡ 1 | x − z | ​ u ​ ( z , t ) ​ d z , u ⁡ ( ⋅ , 0 ) = u 0 ≥ 0 in ​ ℝ 2 . \left\{\begin{aligned} u_{t}=&\;\Delta u-\nabla\cdot(u\nabla v)\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,\infty),\\ v=&\;(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}})^{-1}u:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,\log\frac{1}{|x-z|}\,u(z,t)\,dz,\\ &\qquad\ u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}\geq 0\quad\hbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.\end{aligned}\right. We consider the critical mass case ∫ ℝ 2 u 0 ​ ( x ) ​ 𝑑 x = 8 ​ π \int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}(x)\,dx=8\pi which corresponds to the exact threshold between finite-time blow-up and self-similar diffusion towards zero. We find a radial function u 0 ∗ u_{0}^{*} with mass 8 ​ π 8\pi such that for any initial condition u 0 u_{0} sufficiently close to u 0 ∗ u_{0}^{*} the solution u ⁡ ( x , t ) u(x,t) of ( ∗ ) is globally defined and blows-up in infinite time. As t → + ∞ t\to+\infty it has the approximate profile u ⁡ ( x , t ) ≈ 1 λ 2 ​ U ​ ( x − ξ ⁡ ( t ) λ ⁡ ( t ) ) , U ⁡ ( y ) = 8 ( 1 + | y | 2 ) 2 , u(x,t)\approx\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right),\quad U(y)=\frac{8}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}}, where λ ⁡ ( t ) ≈ c log ⁡ t , ξ ⁡ ( t ) → q \lambda(t)\approx\frac{c}{\sqrt{\log t}},\ \xi(t)\to q for some c > 0 c>0 and q ∈ ℝ 2 q\in\mathbb{R}^{2} . This result answers affirmatively the nonradial stability conjecture raised in [ 26 ] .



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
35K15; 35B40; 35B44

1.
Introduction

This paper deals with the classical Keller-Segel problem in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} u_{t}=&\Delta u-\nabla\cdot(u\nabla v)\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(0,\infty),\\
v=&(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}})^{-1}u:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,\log\frac{1}{|x-z|}\,u(z,t)\,dz,\\
&\qquad\ u(\cdot,0)=u_{0}\quad\hbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

which is a well-known model for the dynamics of a population density
u(x,t)
evolving by diffusion with a chemotactic drift. We consider positive solutions which are well defined, unique and smooth up to a maximal time
0<T\leq+\infty
.
This problem formally preserves mass, in the sense that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,t)dx=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}(x)\,dx=:M\quad\mbox{for all}\quad t\in(0,T).

An interesting feature of (
1.1
) is the connection between the second moment of the solution and its mass which is precisely given by

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}\,u(x,t)\,dx=4M-\frac{M^{2}}{2\pi},

provided that the second moments are finite. If
M>8\pi
, the negative rate of production of the second moment and the positivity of the solution implies finite blow-up time. If
M<8\pi
the solution lives at all times and diffuses to zero with a self similar profile according to
[
5
]
.
When
M=8\pi
the solution is globally defined in time. If the initial second moment is finite, it is preserved in time, and there is
infinite time blow-up
for the solution, as was shown in
[
4
]
.

Globally defined in time solutions of (
1.1
) are of course its positive finite mass steady states, which consist of the family

(1.2)

U_{\lambda,\xi}(x)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U\left(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}\right),\quad U(y)=\frac{8}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}},\quad\lambda>0,\ \xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

We observe that all these steady states have the exact mass
8\pi
and infinite second moment

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U_{\lambda,\xi}(x)\,dx\,=\,8\pi,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}\,U_{\lambda,\xi}(x)\,dx\,=\,+\infty.

As a consequence, if a solution of (
1.1
) is attracted by the family
(U_{\lambda,\xi})
, its mass must be larger than
8\pi
and if the initial second moment is finite, then blow-up occurs in a singular limit corresponding to
\lambda\to 0_{+}
.

In the critical mass
M=8\pi
case, the infinite-time blow-up in (
1.1
) when the second moment is finite, takes place in the form of a bubble in the form (
1.2
) with
\lambda=\lambda(t)\to 0
according to
[
2
,
4
]
. Formal rates and precise profiles were derived in
[
12
,
8
]
to be

\lambda(t)\sim\frac{c}{\sqrt{\log t}}\quad\hbox{as }t\to+\infty.

A radial solution with this rate was built by Ghoul and Masmoudi in
[
26
]
and its stability within the radial class was established. The framework of the construction in
[
26
]
was actually fully nonradial, but for stability a spectral gap inequality only known in the radial case was used. Numerical
evidence for this inequality was obtained in
[
7
]
, and stability for general nonradial perturbation was conjectured in
[
26
]
.
A related spectral estimate, useful in the analysis of finite time blow-up was found in
[
15
]
.

In this paper we construct an infinite-time blow-up solution with a different method to that in
[
26
]
, which in particular leads to a proof of the stability assertion among non-radial functions. The following is our main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

There exists a nonnegative, radially symmetric function
u_{0}^{*}(x)
with critical mass
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}^{*}(x)\,dx=8\pi
and finite second moment
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}\,u_{0}^{*}(x)\,dx<+\infty
such that for every
u_{1}(x)
sufficiently close (in suitable sense) to
u_{0}^{*}
with
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{1}\,dx=8\pi
, we have that the solution
u(x,t)
of system
(\ref{ks})
with initial condition

u(x,0)=u_{1}(x)

has the form

(1.3)

\displaystyle u(x,t)\,=\,\frac{1}{\lambda(t)^{2}}U\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\Bigr)(1+o(1)),\quad U(y)=\frac{8}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}}

uniformly on bounded sets of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and

\lambda(t)\ =\ \frac{c}{\sqrt{\log t}}\,(1+o(1)),\quad\xi(t)\to q\quad\hbox{as }t\to+\infty,

for some number
c>0
and some
q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

Sufficiently close
for the perturbation
u_{1}(x):=u_{0}^{*}(x)+\varphi(x)
in this result is measured in the
C^{1}
-weighted norm for some
\sigma>1

\|\varphi\|_{*\sigma}:=\|(1+|\cdot|^{4+\sigma})\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\|(1+|\cdot|^{5+\sigma}){\nabla}\varphi(x)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<+\infty.

The perturbation
\varphi
must have zero mass too.

“Uniformly on bounded sets” of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in (
1.3
) means that for any bounded
K\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}

\lim_{t\to\infty}\sup_{x\in K}\lambda(t)^{2}U\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\Bigr)^{-1}\left|u(x,t)-\frac{1}{\lambda(t)^{2}}U\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\Bigr)\right|=0.

The expansion of
u(x,t)
can be made more precise though, and this is explained along the proof of theorem.

The scaling parameter is rather simple to find at main order from the approximate conservation of second moment, see Section
2
. The center
\xi(t)
actually obeys a relatively simple system of nonlocal ODEs.

We devote the rest of this paper to the proof of Theorem
1.1
. Our approach borrows elements of constructions in the works
[
16
,
21
,
18
,
17
]
based on the so-called
inner-outer gluing scheme
, where a system is derived for an inner equation defined near the blow-up point and expressed in the variable of the blowing-up bubble, and an outer problem that sees the whole picture in the original scale. The result of Theorem
1.1
has already been announced in
[
20
]
in connection with
[
16
,
21
,
18
]
.

There is a huge literature on chemotaxis in biology and in mathematics. The Patlak-Keller-Segel model
[
44
,
35
]
is used in mathematical biology to describe the motion of mono-cellular organisms, like Dictyostelium Discoideum, which move randomly but experience a drift in presence of a chemo-attractant. Under certain circumstances, these cells are able to emit the chemo-attractant themselves. Through the chemical signal, they coordinate their motion and eventually aggregate. Such a self-organization scenario is at the basis of many models of chemotaxis and is considered as a fundamental mechanism in biology. Of course, the aggregation induced by the drift competes with the noise associated with the random motion so that aggregation occurs only if the chemical signal is strong enough. A classical survey of the mathematical problems in chemotaxis models can be found in
[
31
,
32
]
. After a proper adimensionalization, it turns out that all coefficients in the Patlak-Keller-Segel model studied in this paper can be taken equal to
1
and that the only free parameter left is the total mass. For further considerations on chemotaxis, we shall refer to
[
30
]
for biological models and to
[
11
]
for physics backgrounds.

In many situations of interest, cells are moving on a substrate. The two-dimensional case is therefore of special interest in biology, but also turns out to be particularly interesting from the mathematical point of view as well, because of scaling properties, at least in the simplest versions of the Keller-Segel model. Boundary conditions induce various additional difficulties. In the idealized situation of the Euclidean plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, it is known since the early work of W. Jäger and S. Luckhaus in
[
33
]
that solutions globally exist if the mass
M
is small and blow-up in finite time if
M
is large. The blow-up in a bounded domain is studied in
[
33
,
1
,
39
,
40
,
46
]
. The precise threshold for blow-up,
M=8\pi
, has been determined in
[
23
,
5
]
, with sufficient conditions for global existence if
M\leq 8\pi
in
[
5
]
(also see
[
22
]
in the radial case). The key estimate is the boundedness of the free energy, which relies on the logarithmic Hardy-Littlewood-Sobolev inequality established in optimal form in
[
9
]
. We refer to
[
3
]
for a review of related results. If
M<8\pi
, diffusion dominates: intermediate asymptotic profiles and exact rates of convergence have been determined in
[
7
]
. Also see
[
41
,
25
]
. In the supercritical case
M>8\pi
, various formal expansions are known for many years, starting with
[
27
,
28
,
49
]
which were later justified in
[
45
,
38
]
, in the radial case, and in
[
14
]
, in the non-radially symmetric regime. This latter result is based on the analysis of the spectrum of a linearized operator done in
[
15
]
, based on the earlier work
[
19
]
, and relies on a scalar product already considered in
[
45
]
and similar to the one used in
[
6
,
7
]
in the subcritical mass regime. An interesting subproduct of the blow-up mechanism in
[
45
,
29
]
is that the blow-up takes the form of a concentration in the form of a Dirac distribution with mass exactly
8\pi
at blow-up time, as was expected from
[
29
,
24
]
, but it is still an open question to decide whether this is, locally in space, the only mechanism of blow-up.

The critical mass case
M=8\pi
is more delicate. If the second moment is infinite, there is a variety of behaviors as observed for instance in
[
36
,
37
,
43
]
. For solutions with finite second moment, blow-up is expected to occur as
t\to+\infty
: see
[
34
]
for grow-up rates in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and
[
48
]
for the higher-dimensional radial case. The existence in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of a global radial solution and first results of large time asymptotics were established in
[
2
]
using cumulated mass functions. In
[
4
]
, the infinite time blow-up was proved without symmetry assumptions using the free energy and an assumption of boundedness of the second moment. Also see
[
42
,
43
]
for an existence result under weaker assumptions, and further estimates on the solutions. Asymptotic stability of the family of steady states determined by (
1.2
) under the mass constraint
M=8\pi
has been determined in
[
10
]
. The blow-up rate
\lambda(t)
and the shape of the limiting profile
U
were identified in formal asymptotic expansions in
[
50
,
51
,
47
,
12
,
13
]
and also in
[
8
, Chapter 8]
. As already mentioned, a radial solution with rate
\lambda(t)\sim(\log t)^{-1/2}
was built and its stability within the radial class was established in
[
26
]
.

2.
Formal derivation of the behavior of the parameters

We consider here a first approximation to a solution
u(x,t)
of (
1.1
), globally defined in time, such that on bounded sets in
x
,

(2.1)

u(x,t)=\frac{1}{\lambda(t)^{2}}U\left(\frac{x-\xi(t)}{\lambda(t)}\right)(1+o(1))\quad\text{as }t\to+\infty

for certain functions
0<\lambda(t)\to 0
and
\xi(t)\to q\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, where we recall that

U(y)=\frac{8}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}}.

We know that (
2.1
) can only happen in the critical mass, finite second moment case:

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,t)dx=8\pi,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}u(x,t)dx<+\infty,

which according to the results in
[
4
,
26
,
12
]
is consistent with a behavior of the form (
2.1
). Since the second moment of
U
is infinite, we do not expect the approximation (
2.1
) be uniform in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
but sufficiently far, a faster decay in
x
should take place as we shall see next. We will find an approximate asymptotic expression for the scaling parameter
\lambda(t)
that matches with this behavior.

Let us introduce the function

\Gamma_{0}:=(-\Delta)^{-1}U.

We directly compute

\Gamma_{0}(y)=\log\frac{8}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}}

and hence
\Gamma_{0}
solves the Liouville equation

-\Delta\Gamma_{0}\,=\,e^{\Gamma_{0}}=U{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}.

Then
\nabla\Gamma_{0}(y)\approx-\frac{4y}{|y|^{2}}
for all large
y
, and hence we get, away from
x=\xi
,

-\nabla\cdot(u\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}u)\approx 4\nabla u\cdot\frac{x-\xi}{|x-\xi|^{2}}.

Therefore, defining

(2.2)

\mathcal{E}(u):=\Delta u-\nabla\cdot(u\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}u)

and writing in polar coordinates

u(r,\theta,t)=u(x,t),\quad x=\xi(t)+re^{i\theta},

we find

\mathcal{E}(u)\approx{\partial}_{r}^{2}u+\frac{5}{r}{\partial}_{r}u
.
Hence, assuming that
\dot{\xi}(t)\to 0
sufficiently fast, equation (
1.1
) approximately reads

{\partial}_{t}u={\partial}_{r}^{2}u+\frac{5}{r}{\partial}_{r}u,

which can be idealized as a homogeneous heat equation in
\mathbb{R}^{6}
for radially symmetric functions. It is therefore reasonable to believe
that beyond the self-similar region
r\gg\sqrt{t}
the behavior changes into a function of
r/\sqrt{t}
with fast decay at
+\infty
that yields finiteness of the second moment. To obtain a first global approximation, we simply cut-off the bubble (
2.1
) beyond the self-similar zone.
We introduce a further parameter
\alpha(t)

and set

(2.3)

\displaystyle\bar{u}(x,t)=\frac{\alpha(t)}{\lambda^{2}}U\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}\Bigr)\chi(x,t),

where

(2.4)

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\chi$}}}\chi(x,t)=\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi}{\sqrt{t}}\Bigr)

with
\chi_{0}

a smooth radial cut-off function such that

(2.5)

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\chi_{0}$}}}\chi_{0}(z)=\begin{cases}1\ &\hbox{ if }|z|\leq 1,\\
0\ &\hbox{ if }|z|\geq 2.\end{cases}

We introduce the parameter
\alpha(t)
because the total mass of the actual solution
should equal
8\pi
for all
t
.
But

(2.6)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}\Bigr)\chi(x,t)\,dx=8\pi+16\pi\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}+O\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{4}}{t^{2}}\Bigr),

as
t\to\infty
,
where

(2.7)

\displaystyle\Upsilon=\int_{0}^{\infty}(\tilde{\chi}_{0}(s)-1)s^{-3}ds<0,

and
\chi_{0}(x)=\tilde{\chi}_{0}(|x|)
.
To achieve

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bar{u}(x,t)\,dx=8\pi

we set
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}
where

\displaystyle\bar{\alpha}(t)=1-2\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}+O\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{4}}{t^{2}}\Bigr).

Next we will obtain an approximate value of the scaling parameter
\lambda(t)
that is consistent with the existence of a solution
u(x,t)\approx\bar{u}(x,t)
where
\bar{u}
is the function in (
2.3
) with
\alpha=\bar{\alpha}
.
Let us consider the “error operator”

(2.8)

S(u)=-u_{t}+\mathcal{E}(u),

where
\mathcal{E}(u)
is defined in (
2.2
).
We have the following well-known identities,
valid for an arbitrary function
\omega(x)
of class
C^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with finite mass
and
D^{2}\omega(x)=O(|x|^{-4-\sigma})
for large
|x|
.
We have

(2.9)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{|}x|^{2}\mathcal{E}(\omega)\,dx=4M-\frac{M^{2}}{2\pi},\quad M=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega(x)dx

and

(2.10)

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x\mathcal{E}(\omega)\,dx=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{E}(\omega)\,dx=0.

Let us recall the simple proof of (
2.9
). Integrating by parts on finite balls with large radii and using the behavior of the boundary terms we get
the identities

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathcal{|}x|^{2}\Delta\omega\,dx

\displaystyle=4M,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x|^{2}\nabla\cdot(\omega\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1})\omega)\,dx

\displaystyle=-2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}x\cdot\omega\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\omega\,dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega(x)\omega(y)\frac{x\cdot(x-y)}{|x-y|^{2}}dx\,dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\omega(x)\omega(y)\frac{(x-y)\cdot(x-y)}{|x-y|^{2}}dx\,dy

(2.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{M^{2}}{2\pi}

and then (
2.9
) follows. The proof of (
2.10
) is even simpler. For a solution
u(x,t)
of (
1.1
) we then get

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,t)|x|^{2}dx=4M-\frac{M^{2}}{2\pi},\quad M=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u(x,t)dx.

In particular, if
u(x,t)
is sufficiently close to
\bar{u}(x,t)
and since
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bar{u}(x,t)dx=8\pi
, we get the approximate validity of the identity

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\bar{u}(x,t)|x|^{2}dx=0.

This means

aI(t):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{\bar{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}U\left(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}\right)\chi_{0}\left(\frac{x-\xi}{\sqrt{t}}\right)|x|^{2}dx\,=\,constant.

We readily check that for some constant
\kappa

I(t)=16\pi\lambda^{2}\int_{0}^{\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\lambda}}\frac{\rho^{3}d\rho}{(1+\rho^{2})^{2}}+\kappa+o(1)=16\pi\lambda^{2}\log\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\lambda}+\kappa+o(1)\quad\text{as }\lambda\to 0.

Then we conclude that
\lambda(t)
approximately satisfies

\lambda^{2}\log t\,=\,c^{2}\,=\,constant

and hence we get at main order

\lambda(t)=\frac{c}{\sqrt{\log t}}.

We also notice that the center of mass is preserved for a true solution, thanks to (
2.10
):

\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}xu(x,t)dx=0.

Since the center of mass of
\bar{u}(x,t)
is exactly
\xi(t)
we then get that approximately

\xi(t)=constant=q.

3.
The approximations
u_{0}
and
u_{1}

From now on we to consider the Keller-Segel system starting at a large
t_{0}
:

(3.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} u_{t}=&\Delta u-\nabla\cdot(u\nabla v)\quad{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty),\\
v=&(-\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}})^{-1}u:=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\,\log\frac{1}{|x-z|}\,u(z,t)\,dz,\\
&\qquad\ u(\cdot,t_{0})=u_{0}\quad\hbox{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

which is equivalent to (
1.1
). We do this so that some expansions for
t
large take a simpler form.

In this section we will define a basic approximation to a solution of the Keller-Segel system (
3.1
). Let us consider parameter functions

0<\lambda(t)\to 0,\quad\xi(t)\to q,\quad\alpha(t)\to 1\quad\hbox{as }t\to+\infty

that we will later specify.
Let us consider the functions

U(y)=\frac{8}{(1+|y|^{2})^{2}},\quad\Gamma_{0}(y)=\log U(y)

and define the approximate solution
u_{0}(x,t)
as

(3.2)

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$u_{0}$}}}u_{0}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}\Bigr)\chi(x,t),

\displaystyle v_{0}(x,t)

\displaystyle=(-\Delta_{x})^{-1}u_{0}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\log\frac{1}{|x-\bar{x}|}\,u_{0}(\bar{x},t)\,d\bar{x},

where
\chi
is the cut-off function (
5.3
).

We consider the error operator

\displaystyle S(u)

\displaystyle=-{\partial}_{t}u+\mathcal{E}(u),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u)=\Delta_{x}u-\nabla_{x}\cdot(u\nabla_{x}v),\quad v=(-\Delta_{x})^{-1}u.

and next measure the error of approximation
S(u_{0})
.

We have

\displaystyle-{\partial}_{t}u_{0}(x,t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}U(y)\chi_{0}(z)+\alpha\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda^{3}}Z_{0}\chi_{0}(z)+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{3}}\dot{\xi}\cdot{\nabla}_{y}U(y)\,\chi_{0}(z)

(3.3)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{t}}U(y)\dot{\xi}\cdot\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)+\frac{\alpha}{2\lambda^{2}t}U(y)\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot z,

z=\frac{x-\xi}{\sqrt{t}}

where

(3.4)

\displaystyle Z_{0}(y)=2U(y)+y\cdot\nabla_{y}U(y),\quad y=\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}.

We also have

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\Delta_{x}u_{0}-\nabla_{x}\cdot(u_{0}\nabla_{x}v_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda^{3}t^{1/2}}\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot\nabla_{y}U(y)+\frac{\alpha}{t}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\Delta_{z}\chi_{0}(z)U(y)-\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{t}}U(y)\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{0}

\displaystyle+\frac{\alpha\chi_{0}(z)}{\lambda^{4}}\Bigl[(\chi_{0}(z)\alpha-1)U^{2}(y)-\nabla_{y}U(y)\cdot(\nabla_{y}v_{0}-\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0})\Bigr]\ .

Let us decompose

(3.5)

v_{0}(y)=\alpha\Gamma_{0}(y)+\mathcal{R}(y).

For the term
\mathcal{R}
in (
3.5
) we directly estimate

(3.6)

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}\mathcal{R}(y)|\leq\begin{cases}\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\frac{1}{|y|}&|y|\geq\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\lambda},\\
0&|y|\leq\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\lambda}.\end{cases}

Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda^{3}t^{1/2}}\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot\nabla_{y}U(y)+\frac{\alpha}{t}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\Delta_{z}\chi_{0}(z)U(y)-\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{t}}U(y)\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\nabla_{x}v_{0}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha\chi_{0}(z)}{\lambda^{4}}\Bigl[(\alpha-1)U^{2}(y)-(\alpha-1)\nabla_{y}U(y)\cdot\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0}(y)+\alpha(\chi_{0}(z)-1)U^{2}(y)

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla_{y}U(y)\cdot\nabla_{y}\mathcal{R}(y)\Bigr].

and thus

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$S(u_{0})$}}}S(u_{0})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}U(y)\chi_{0}(z)+\alpha\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda^{3}}Z_{0}\chi_{0}(z)+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{3}}\dot{\xi}\cdot{\nabla}_{y}U(y)\,\chi_{0}(z)

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{t}}U(y)\dot{\xi}\cdot\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)+\frac{\alpha}{2\lambda^{2}t}U(y)\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot z

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2\alpha}{\lambda^{3}t^{1/2}}\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot\nabla_{y}U(y)+\frac{\alpha}{t}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\Delta_{z}\chi_{0}(z)U(y)-\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{t}}U(y)\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot\nabla_{x}v_{0}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)\chi_{0}(z)}{\lambda^{4}}\nabla_{y}\cdot(U(y)\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0}(y))

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha\chi_{0}(z)}{\lambda^{4}}\Bigl[\alpha(\chi-1)U^{2}(y)-\nabla_{y}U(y)\cdot\nabla_{y}\mathcal{R}(y)\Bigr].

For a function
v(\zeta)
defined for
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
consider the operator

(3.7)

\displaystyle\Delta_{6}v(\zeta)=\Delta v(\zeta)+4\frac{\zeta}{|\zeta|^{2}}\cdot{\nabla}_{\zeta}v(\zeta).

The reason for the notation is that for radial functions
v=v(r)
,
r=|\zeta|
, we have

\Delta_{6}v={\partial}^{2}_{r}v+\frac{5}{r}{\partial}_{r}v,

which corresponds to Laplace’s operator in
\mathbb{R}^{6}
on radial functions.

Let
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(\zeta,t)
be the (radial) solution to

(3.8)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}&=\Delta_{6}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}+E(\zeta,t)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\frac{t_{0}}{2},\infty),\\
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(\cdot,\frac{t_{0}}{2})&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

given by Duhamel’s formula,
where
E(\zeta,t)
is the radial function

(3.9)

\displaystyle E(\zeta,t;\lambda)=\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda^{3}}Z_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\zeta}{\lambda}\Bigr)\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{t}}\Bigr)+\frac{1}{2\lambda^{2}t}U\Bigl(\frac{\zeta}{\lambda}\Bigr)\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot z+\tilde{E}(x,t),

and

\displaystyle\tilde{E}(\zeta,t;\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\lambda^{3}t^{1/2}}\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot\nabla_{y}U(y)+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}t}\Delta_{z}\chi_{0}(z)U(y)

(3.10)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}t^{1/2}}U(y)\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}(z)\cdot\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0}(y),

with
z=\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{t}}
,
y=\frac{\zeta}{\lambda}
.

We then define

(3.11)

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$\varphi_{\lambda}$}}}\varphi_{\lambda}(x,t)=\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(x-\xi(t),t).

The reason to define
\varphi_{\lambda}
for
t>\frac{t_{0}}{2}
is that it gives better properties for the first approximation of
\lambda
constructed in Section
7
.
Since
\lambda(t)
is defined naturally for
t>t_{0}
, we will need to define
\lambda(t)
for
\frac{t_{0}}{2}<t<t_{0}
in an appropriate way (see Proposition
5.1
and Section
7
).
We will write
\lambda=\lambda_{0}+\lambda_{1}
where both of these functions are constructed so that they are defined for
t>\frac{t_{0}}{2}
. The construction of
\lambda_{0}
is given in Proposition
5.1
.
In particular
\lambda_{0}(t)=\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{\log t}}(1+o(1))
as
t\to\infty
.
Note that
\varphi_{\lambda}(\cdot,t_{0})
is not zero.

We define the approximate solution

(3.12)

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$u_{1}$}}}u_{1}:=u_{0}+\varphi_{\lambda}

which depends on the parameter functions
\alpha(t)
,
\xi(t)
,
\lambda(t)
. Correspondingly, we write

v_{1}\ :=\ (-\Delta_{x})^{-1}(u_{1})\,.

We will establish in the next sections that a suitable choice of these functions makes it possible to find an actual solution of (
3.1
) as a lower order perturbation of
u_{1}
.

4.
The first error of approximation

We will assume the following conditions on
\lambda
,
\alpha
,
\xi

(4.1)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &|\lambda(t)|+t\log(t)|\dot{\lambda}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{\sqrt{\log(t)}}\\
&|\dot{\xi}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{\gamma}}\\
&|\alpha(t)-1|\leq\frac{C}{t\log t},\quad|\dot{\alpha}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{2}\,\log t},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\frac{3}{2}<\gamma<2
.

We compute

\displaystyle S(u_{1})

\displaystyle=S(u_{0}+\varphi_{\lambda})=S(u_{0})-\partial_{t}\varphi_{\lambda}+\mathcal{L}_{u_{0}}[\varphi_{\lambda}]-\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi_{\lambda}).

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{u_{0}}[\varphi]

\displaystyle=\Delta\varphi-\nabla\cdot(\varphi\nabla v_{0})-\nabla\cdot(u_{0}\nabla\psi),

\displaystyle\psi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=(-\Delta)^{-1}\varphi_{\lambda},\quad v_{0}=(-\Delta)^{-1}u_{0}.

Then

\displaystyle S(u_{1})

\displaystyle=-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}U(y)\chi+(\alpha-1)\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda^{3}}Z_{0}\chi+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{3}}\dot{\xi}\cdot{\nabla}_{y}U(y)\,\chi+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{t}}U(y)\dot{\xi}\cdot\nabla\chi_{0}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(\alpha-1)}{2t}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}\cdot\frac{x-\xi}{\sqrt{t}}+\frac{2(\alpha-1)}{\lambda^{3}t^{1/2}}\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{y}U

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{(\alpha-1)}{t}\Delta\chi_{0}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U-\frac{\alpha^{2}-1}{\lambda^{3}\sqrt{t}}U\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0}-\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{3}\sqrt{t}}U\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{y}\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)\chi}{\lambda^{4}}\nabla_{y}\cdot(U\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0})+\frac{\alpha^{2}\chi(1-\chi)}{\lambda^{4}}U^{2}-\frac{\alpha\chi}{\lambda^{4}}\nabla_{y}U\cdot\nabla_{y}\mathcal{R}

(4.2)

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}\cdot\dot{\xi}-\frac{4}{r}\partial_{r}\varphi_{\lambda}-\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla v_{0})-\nabla\cdot(u_{0}\nabla\psi_{\lambda})-\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi_{\lambda}),

where
\mathcal{R}
is defined in the decomposition (
3.5
).

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
\varphi_{\lambda}
be defined by (
3.11
)-(
3.8
) with
\lambda
satisfying (
4.1
).
Then

(4.3)

\displaystyle|\varphi_{\lambda}(x,t)|+(|x-\xi|+\lambda)|\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}(x,t)|\leq C\frac{1}{t\log t}\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}+|x-\xi|^{2}}&|x-\xi|\leq\sqrt{t}\\
\frac{1}{t}e^{-\frac{|x-\xi|^{2}}{4t}}&|x-\xi|\geq\sqrt{t}.\end{cases}

We also have

(4.4)

\displaystyle|\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}(x,t)|\leq\frac{C}{t\log t}\frac{|x-\xi|}{(\lambda+|x-\xi|)^{4}},\quad|x-\xi|\leq\sqrt{t}.

Proof.

In terms of the function
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}
defined in (
3.8
), with
r=|x-\xi|
we claim that

\displaystyle|\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(r,t)|\leq C\frac{1}{t\log t}\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}+r^{2}}&r\leq\sqrt{t},\\
\frac{1}{t}e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{4t}}&r\geq\sqrt{t}.\end{cases}

For the proof of this we use barriers.
Consider

\psi_{1}(r,t)=\frac{1}{t\log t}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}+r^{2}}

and note that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi_{1}-\Bigl(\partial_{rr}+\frac{5}{r}\partial_{r}\Bigr)\psi_{1}\geq c\frac{\lambda^{-4}}{t\log t(1+r/\lambda)^{4}},\quad r\leq 2\delta\sqrt{t}

for some
c>0
,
\delta>0
.

Let
\chi_{\delta\sqrt{t}}(r,t)=\tilde{\chi}_{0}(\frac{r}{\delta\sqrt{t}})
where
\tilde{\chi}_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is such that
\tilde{\chi}_{0}(s)=1
for
s\leq 1
and
\tilde{\chi}_{0}(s)=0
for
s\geq 2
.
Consider

\displaystyle\psi(r,t)=\psi_{1}(r,t)\chi_{\delta\sqrt{t}}(r,t)+\frac{C_{1}}{t^{2}\log t}e^{-\frac{r^{2}}{4t}}.

The function
\tilde{E}
(
3.10
) can be estimated by

\displaystyle|\tilde{E}(\zeta,t)|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}t^{3}}h_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{t}}\Bigr)

where
h_{1}(z)
is a smooth function with compact support.
Then
E
(
3.9
) has the estimate

\displaystyle|\tilde{E}(\zeta,t)|\leq C\frac{|\lambda\dot{\lambda}|}{(r^{2}+\lambda^{2})^{2}}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}t^{3}}h_{2}\Bigl(\frac{\zeta}{\sqrt{t}}\Bigr)

where
h_{2}(z)
is a smooth function with compact support.

Then for
C_{1}
sufficiently large

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi-\Bigl(\partial_{rr}+\frac{5}{r}\partial_{r}\Bigr)\psi\geq c|E(r,t)|,

where
c>0
.

By the comparison principle,

|\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(r,t)|\leq C\psi(r,t),

for some uniform constant
C
.
After a suitable scaling,
from standard parabolic estimates we also get

\displaystyle(\lambda+r)|\nabla_{x}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(r,t)|\leq C\psi(r,t).

With these two inequalities we obtain (
4.3
).

To prove (
4.4
) we change variables
y=\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}
in the equation (
3.8
) and define

\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(r,t)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}\Bigl(\frac{r}{\lambda},t\Bigr).

We get the equation, after interpreting
\rho=|y|
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{6}

\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\hat{\varphi}=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\hat{\varphi}+\lambda\dot{\lambda}(2\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}+y\cdot\nabla_{y}\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda})+\lambda^{4}E(\lambda y,t),

where
E
is defined in (
3.9
). Differentiating with respect to
y
and using the bound we already have for
\nabla_{y}\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}
from (
4.4
), and using standard parabolic estimates, we get

\displaystyle|D^{2}_{y}\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}(y,t)|\leq\frac{C}{t\log t}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{4}},\quad|y|\leq\sqrt{t\log t}.

Using that
\nabla\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}(0,t)=0
we deduce
that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{y}\hat{\varphi}_{\lambda}(y,t)|\leq\frac{C}{t\log t}\frac{|y|}{(1+|y|)^{4}},\quad|y|\leq\sqrt{t\log t},

which readily gives (
4.4
).

∎

Lemma 4.2
.

Assuming (
4.1
) we have

(4.5)

\displaystyle\lambda^{4}|S(u_{1})|\chi(x,t)\leq C\frac{1}{t\,\log t}\frac{\log(2+|y|)}{1+|y|^{6}},\quad y=\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda},

and

(4.6)

\displaystyle|S(u_{1})|(1-\chi)\leq C\frac{1}{t^{4}\log t}e^{-c\frac{|x|^{2}}{t}},

for some
c\in(0,\frac{1}{4})
.

Proof.

Let us analyze the terms involving
\varphi_{\lambda}
.
We estimate, using Lemma
4.1
,

\displaystyle\left|\lambda^{2}U(y)\varphi_{\lambda}(\xi+\lambda y)\right|\leq C\frac{1}{t\log t}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{6}},\quad|y|\leq\sqrt{t\log t}.

Similarly, by (
3.5
)

\displaystyle-\frac{4}{r}\partial_{r}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}-\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}\cdot\nabla v_{0}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{r}\partial_{r}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}-\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}-(\alpha-1)\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}-\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}\cdot\nabla\mathcal{R}

(4.7)

\displaystyle=4\Bigr(\frac{r}{r^{2}+\lambda^{2}}-\frac{1}{r}\Bigl)\partial_{r}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}-(\alpha-1)\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}-\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}\cdot\nabla\mathcal{R}.

By (
4.4
)

\displaystyle\Bigl|\lambda^{4}4\Bigr(\frac{r}{r^{2}+\lambda^{2}}-\frac{1}{r}\Bigl)\partial_{r}\varphi_{\lambda}\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{t\log t}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{6}},\quad|y|\leq\sqrt{t\log t}.

The other terms in (
4.7
) are estimated similarly, using the hypotheses on
\alpha
and the estimate on
\mathcal{R}
(
3.6
), and we get

\displaystyle\left|-\frac{4}{r}\partial_{r}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}-\nabla\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}\cdot\nabla v_{0}\right|\leq\frac{C}{t\log t}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{6}},\quad|y|\leq\sqrt{t\log t}.

The terms involving
\psi_{\lambda}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\varphi_{\lambda}
are estimated using the formula

\displaystyle\partial_{r}\psi_{\lambda}(r,t)=\frac{1}{r}\int_{0}^{r}\varphi_{\lambda}(s,t)sds.

In
\lambda^{4}S(u_{1})
we have also the term
-\dot{\alpha}\lambda^{2}U(y)\chi
, which thanks to (
4.1
) can be estimated as

\displaystyle\Bigl|\lambda^{2}\dot{\alpha}U(y)\chi\Bigr|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C\lambda^{2}}{t^{2}\log t}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{4}}\chi(y,t)\leq\frac{C}{t\log t}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{6}}\chi(y,t).

The remaining terms are estimated similarly, and we obtain (
4.5
).

The stated inequality (
4.6
) follows from the Gaussian decay of
\varphi_{\lambda}
in Lemma
4.1
.
∎

5.
The inner-outer gluing system

Let us consider the initial approximation

u_{1}(x,t)=u_{0}(x,t)+\varphi_{\lambda}(x,t)

built in Section
3

for a given choice of the parameter functions
\lambda(t)
,
\alpha(t)
,
\xi(t)
satisfying (
4.1
). Here

u_{0}
is the function defined in (
3.2
) and
\varphi_{\lambda}
that in (
3.11
).
We look for a solution of the Keller-Segel equation (
3.1
)
in the form of a small perturbation of
u_{1}
,
namely

(5.1)

\displaystyle u(x,t)=u_{1}(x,t)+\Phi(x,t).

We write the perturbation
\Phi
as a sum of an “inner” contribution, better expressed in the scale of
u_{0}
, and
a remote effect that takes into consideration the “outer” regime.
Precisely, we write

(5.2)

\displaystyle\Phi(x,t)=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}(y,t)\chi(x,t)+\varphi^{o}(x,t),\quad y=\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda},

where
\chi
is the smooth cut-off

(5.3)

\displaystyle\chi(x,t)=\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi}{\sqrt{t}}\Bigr)

with
\chi_{0}
a smooth radial cut-off function such that
\chi_{0}(z)=1
if
|z|\leq 1
,
\chi_{0}(z)=1
if
|z|\geq 2
.
(The same as defined in (
2.4
).)

Recall
S(u)
given by

S(u)=-\partial_{t}u+\Delta u-\nabla\cdot(u\nabla v),\quad v=(-\Delta)^{-1}u,

where the operators act on the original variable
x
unless otherwise indicated.
In the computations that follow we will express the equation

S(u_{1}+\Phi)=0

for
\Phi
given by (
5.2
), as a parabolic system in its inner and outer contributions
\phi^{i}
and

\varphi^{o}
. The coupling in that system will be small if
\phi^{i}(y,t)
decays sufficiently fast in space and time. That can only be achieved for suitable choices of the parameters
\alpha,\lambda,\xi
that yield certain solvability conditions
satisfied. The set of all these relations is what we call the inner-outer gluing system.
Next we formulate this system. It will be necessary to successively refine its original expression by further decomposing
\phi^{i}
into two contributions with separate space decay, finally arriving at the equations
(
5.47
), (
5.48
), (
5.49
) and
(
5.51
) which are the ones we will actually solve.

Let us observe that

\displaystyle S(u_{1}+\Phi)

\displaystyle=S(u_{1})-\partial_{t}\Bigl(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\chi\Bigr)-\partial_{t}\varphi^{o}+\mathcal{L}_{u_{1}}\Bigr[\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\chi\Bigl]+\mathcal{L}_{u_{1}}[\varphi^{o}]

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla\cdot(\Phi\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\Phi),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{u_{1}}[\varphi]

\displaystyle=\Delta\varphi-\nabla\cdot(\varphi\nabla v_{1})-\nabla\cdot(u_{1}\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\varphi),\qquad v_{1}=(-\Delta)^{-1}u_{1}.

We use the notation

\psi=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi^{i},\quad\hat{\psi}=\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}(-\Delta)^{-1}(\phi^{i}\chi),

in the expressions that follow.
We expand

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{u_{1}}[\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\chi]

\displaystyle=\chi\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\Delta\phi^{i}+\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\phi^{i}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\Delta\chi-\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\chi\nabla v_{1})-\nabla\cdot(u_{1}\nabla\hat{\psi}).

We have

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(u_{1}\nabla\hat{\psi})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla\psi)\chi+\nabla\cdot(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla(\hat{\psi}-\psi))\chi+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\hat{\psi}

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi)+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}{\nabla}(\hat{\psi}-\psi))

and

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\chi\nabla v_{1})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla v_{1})\chi+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla v_{1}.

Recall the notation

\displaystyle v_{1}=v_{0}+\psi_{\lambda},\quad v_{0}=\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}(-\Delta)^{-1}(U\chi),\quad\psi_{\lambda}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\varphi_{\lambda},

and also (
3.5
)

v_{0}=\alpha\Gamma_{0}+\mathcal{R},\quad\mathcal{R}=\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}(-\Delta)^{-1}\bigl(U(\chi-1)\bigl).

Then

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\chi\nabla v_{1})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla v_{0})\chi+\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\psi_{\lambda})\chi+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla v_{0}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\psi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}\nabla\cdot(\phi^{i}\nabla\Gamma_{0})\chi+\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\mathcal{R})\chi+\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\psi_{\lambda})\chi

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\mathcal{R}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\psi_{\lambda}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{u_{1}}[\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\chi]

\displaystyle=\chi\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\Delta\phi^{i}+\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\phi^{i}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\Delta\chi

\displaystyle\quad-\Bigl[\nabla\cdot(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\Gamma_{0})\chi+\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\mathcal{R})\chi+\nabla\cdot(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\psi_{\lambda})\chi

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\mathcal{R}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\psi_{\lambda}\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad-\Bigl[\nabla\cdot(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla\psi)\chi+\nabla\cdot(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla(\hat{\psi}-\psi))\chi+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\hat{\psi}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi)+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla(\hat{\psi}-\psi))\Bigr].

Next we expand

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{u_{1}}[\varphi^{o}]

\displaystyle=\Delta\varphi^{o}-\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla v_{1})-\nabla\cdot(u_{1}\nabla\psi^{o}),\quad\psi^{o}=(-\Delta)^{-1}\varphi^{o}.

We have

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(u_{1}\nabla\psi^{o})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\chi\nabla\psi^{o})+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi^{o})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla\psi^{o})\chi+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\psi^{o}+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi^{o})\chi

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi^{o})(1-\chi),

and

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla v_{1})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla v_{0})+\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\psi_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\alpha\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\Gamma_{0})+\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\mathcal{R})+\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\psi_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\nabla\varphi^{o}\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U\varphi^{o}+(\alpha-1)\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\mathcal{R})+\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\psi_{\lambda}).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{u_{1}}[\varphi^{o}]

\displaystyle=\Delta\varphi^{o}-\Bigl[\nabla\cdot(\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla\psi^{o})\chi+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\psi^{o}+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi^{o})\chi

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi^{o})(1-\chi)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad-\Bigl[\nabla\varphi^{o}\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U\varphi^{o}+(\alpha-1)\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\quad+\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\mathcal{R})+\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\psi_{\lambda})\Bigr].

Based on the previous formulas we formulate the inner equation

\displaystyle\lambda^{4}\partial_{t}(\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i})

\displaystyle=L[\phi^{i}]-(\alpha-1)\nabla_{y}\cdot(U\nabla_{y}\psi)-(\alpha-1)\nabla_{y}\cdot(\phi^{i}\nabla\Gamma_{0})+\lambda^{4}S(u_{1})

\displaystyle\quad-\lambda^{2}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla_{y}\psi^{o})-\lambda^{2}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla_{y}\psi_{\lambda})+\lambda^{2}U\varphi^{o}-\alpha\nabla_{y}\cdot(U\nabla_{y}\psi^{o})

\displaystyle\quad-\lambda^{2}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla_{y}\psi)-\nabla_{y}\cdot(\phi^{i}\nabla_{y}\psi_{\lambda})-(\alpha-1)\lambda^{2}\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\quad-\alpha\nabla_{y}\cdot(U\nabla_{y}(\hat{\psi}-\psi))-\lambda^{2}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla_{y}(\hat{\psi}-\psi))-\nabla_{y}\cdot((\phi^{i}\chi+\lambda^{2}\varphi^{o})\nabla_{y}(\hat{\psi}+\psi^{o})),

where

(5.4)

\displaystyle{\@index{\@indexphrase{$L$, linerized operator around $U$}}}L[\phi]=\Delta_{y}\phi-\nabla_{y}\cdot(U\nabla_{y}\psi)-\nabla_{y}\cdot(\phi\nabla\Gamma_{0}).

We slightly modify the inner equation into the form

(5.5)

\displaystyle\lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\phi^{i}

\displaystyle=L[\phi^{i}]+B_{0}[\phi^{i}]+E_{1}\tilde{\chi}+F(\phi^{i},\varphi^{o},\textbf{p})\tilde{\chi}

where

\textbf{p}=(\lambda,\alpha,\xi),

\displaystyle E_{1}(y,t)=\lambda^{4}S(u_{1}(\textbf{p}))(x,t),\quad y=\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda},

\displaystyle F(\phi^{i},\varphi^{o},\textbf{p})

\displaystyle=-\lambda^{2}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla_{y}\psi^{o})-\lambda^{2}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla_{y}\psi_{\lambda})+\lambda^{2}U\varphi^{o}

\displaystyle\quad-(\alpha-1)\lambda^{2}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0})-\alpha\nabla_{y}\cdot(U\nabla_{y}\psi^{o})

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda\dot{\xi}\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi^{i}-\lambda^{2}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla_{y}\psi)-\nabla_{y}\cdot(\phi^{i}\nabla_{y}\psi_{\lambda})

\displaystyle\quad-(\alpha-1)\nabla_{y}\cdot(U\nabla_{y}\psi)-(\alpha-1)\nabla_{y}\cdot(\phi^{i}\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0})

\displaystyle\quad-\alpha\nabla_{y}\cdot(U\nabla_{y}(\hat{\psi}-\psi))-\lambda^{2}\nabla_{y}\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla_{y}(\hat{\psi}-\psi))

(5.6)

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla_{y}\cdot((\phi^{i}\chi+\lambda^{2}\varphi^{o})\nabla_{y}(\hat{\psi}+\psi^{o})),\qquad\hat{\psi}=(-\Delta_{y})^{-1}(\phi^{i}\chi),

(5.7)

\displaystyle B_{0}[\phi^{i}]

\displaystyle=\lambda\dot{\lambda}(2\phi^{i}+y\cdot\nabla_{y}\phi^{i}),

and

(5.8)

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}(y,t)=\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda y}{2\sqrt{t}}\Bigr),

with
\chi_{0}
as in (
2.5
).
Similarly we formulate the outer equation as

(5.9)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\varphi^{o}

\displaystyle=\Delta\varphi^{o}-\nabla\Gamma_{0}\cdot\nabla\varphi^{o}+G(\phi^{i},\varphi^{o},\mathbf{p})

where

\displaystyle G(\phi^{i},\varphi^{o},\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=S(u_{1},\textbf{p})(1-\chi)+\frac{2}{\lambda^{2}}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\phi^{i}+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\Delta\chi-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\partial_{t}\chi-\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U\varphi^{o}(1-\chi)-\alpha\lambda^{2}U\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\psi^{o}-\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi^{o})(1-\chi)

\displaystyle\quad-(\alpha-1)\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\Gamma_{0})(1-\chi)-\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\mathcal{R})-\nabla\cdot(\varphi^{o}\nabla\psi_{\lambda})(1-\chi)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\nabla\cdot(\phi^{i}\nabla\mathcal{R})\chi-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\mathcal{R}-\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\psi_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla\chi\cdot\nabla\hat{\psi}-\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla(\hat{\psi}-\psi))(1-\chi)

(5.10)

\displaystyle\quad-\nabla(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi)(1-\chi)-\nabla\cdot((\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}\phi^{i}\chi+\varphi^{o})\nabla(\hat{\psi}+\psi^{o}))(1-\chi).

If
\phi^{i}
,
\varphi^{o}
is a solution to system (
5.5
), (
5.9
), then
u
given by (
5.1
), (
5.2
) satisfies the Keller-Segel system (
3.1
).

5.1.
Choice of
\lambda_{0}
and
\alpha_{0}

We explain the choice of
\lambda_{0}
in the context of the elliptic equation

(5.11)

\displaystyle L[\phi]=h\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},

where
h
is radial.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
h(y)
be a radial function such that

\displaystyle\|(1+|y|)^{\gamma}h(y)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\infty,

for some
\gamma>4

and satisfying

(5.12)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)dy=0

(5.13)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)|y|^{2}dy=0.

Then there exists a radial solution
\phi(y)
of equation (
5.11
) such that

(5.14)

\displaystyle|\phi(y)|\leq C\|(1+|y|)^{\gamma}h(y)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{\gamma-2}},\quad\text{if }\gamma\not=6

(5.15)

\displaystyle|\phi(y)|\leq C\|(1+|y|)^{\gamma}h(y)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\frac{\log(1+|y|)}{(1+|y|)^{4}},\quad\text{if }\gamma=6,

and

(5.16)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi(y)dy=0.

Proof.

Defining
g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi
we obtain the equation

(5.17)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(U\nabla g)=h.

Assuming
\gamma>6
we choose the radial function
g
defined by

g(\rho)=-\int_{\rho}^{\infty}\frac{1}{rU(r)}\int_{0}^{r}h(s)sdsdr,\quad\rho=|y|,

and using (
5.12
) we get

|g(\rho)|\leq C\|(1+|y|)^{\gamma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{\gamma-6}}.

Now we solve Liouville’s equation

(5.18)

\displaystyle-\Delta\psi-U\psi=Ug\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\psi(\rho)\to 0\quad\text{as }\rho\to\infty.

Multiplying (
5.17
) by
|y|^{2}
and using (
5.13
) we see that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}gZ_{0}dy=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)|y|^{2}dy=0,

with
Z_{0}
defined in (
3.4
).
Then by the variations of parameter formula we find that (
5.18
) has a unique solution
\psi
, which satisfies

(5.19)

\displaystyle|\psi(y)|+(1+|y|)|\nabla\psi(y)|\leq\|(1+|y|)^{\gamma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{\gamma-4}}.

Then we see that
\phi
defined by
\phi=Ug+U\psi
satisfies (
5.11
), (
5.14
)
and (
5.16
)
because
\phi=-\Delta\psi
and
\psi
has the decay (
5.19
).

If
4<\gamma\leq 6
we do almost the same, except that we define

g(\rho)=\int_{0}^{\rho}\frac{1}{rU(r)}\int_{0}^{r}h(s)sdsdr.

∎

Remark 5.1
.

We observe that
L[Z_{0}]=0
.
This can also be seen in the context of the Lemma
5.1
, where
\phi=Z_{0}
which corresponds to
g
being constant. Indeed, suppose
g\equiv 1
. Then from (
5.18
)
\psi=-1-\frac{1}{2}z_{0}
, where
z_{0}
is defined in (
9.2
).
This gives
\phi=Ug+U\psi=-\frac{1}{U}z_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}Z_{0}
. This shows that
L[Z_{0}]=0
.

If
h
doesn’t satisfy the zero second moment condition (
5.13
), then a solution still exists but with worse decay and non-zero mass.
More precisely, if
h
is radial,
\|(1+|y|)^{\gamma}h(y)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<\infty

for some
\gamma>6
, and satisfies only (
5.12
), then one can construct a solution
\phi
to (
5.11
), but any such solution has the estimate

|\phi(y)|\leq C\|(1+|y|)^{\gamma}h(y)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\frac{\log(1+|y|)}{(1+|y|)^{4}},

so worse decay than the one in (
5.14
).
Moreover, the mass of
\phi
becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Delta\psi=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}gZ_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)|y|^{2}dy.

For the inner equation (
5.5
) it is then natural to impose that the first error
S(u_{1})\chi
satisfies the second moment condition

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1})\chi|y|^{2}dy=0,\quad\text{for all }t>t_{0}.

The next lemma gives a way of expressing the second moment of
u_{1}
.

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
u_{1}
be defined in (
3.12
). Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1})|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}(x-\xi,t;\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}\,dx\cdot\dot{\xi}-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U\chi|x-\xi|^{2}dx-(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t;\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}dx

(5.20)

\displaystyle\quad+4\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}\Bigr).

where
E
,
\tilde{E}
are defined in (
3.9
), (
3.10
).

Proof of Lemma
5.2
.

Using (
2.11
) we see that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1})|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{t}u_{0}|x-\xi|^{2}dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{\lambda}|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad+4\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}\Bigr).

But recall that
\varphi_{\lambda}(x,t)=\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}(x-\xi(t),t)
where
\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}
satisfies (
3.8
). Multiplying that equation by
|\zeta|^{2}
and integrating on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
results in

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{t}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}|\zeta|^{2}\,d\zeta=-4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}\,d\zeta+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(\zeta,t)|\zeta|^{2}\,d\zeta.

Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{t}\varphi_{\lambda}|x-\xi|^{2}\,dx=-4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}\,dx-\frac{(x-\xi)\cdot\dot{\xi}}{|x-\xi|}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{r}\tilde{\varphi}_{\lambda}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(\zeta,t)|\zeta|^{2}\,d\zeta

and then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1})|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{t}u_{0}|x-\xi|^{2}dx+4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}\,dx\cdot\dot{\xi}-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t)|x-\xi|^{2}dx

(5.21)

\displaystyle\quad+4\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}\Bigr).

But from the formula for
\partial_{t}u_{0}
(
3.3
) and the definitions of
E
and
\tilde{E}
(
3.9
), (
3.10
) we get

\displaystyle-\partial_{t}u_{0}(x,t)=-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}U(y)\chi_{0}(z)+\alpha E(x-\xi,t)-\alpha\tilde{E}(x-\xi,t).

Hence

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_{t}u_{0}+E(x-\xi,t))|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\partial_{t}u_{0}+\alpha E(x-\xi))|x-\xi|^{2}dx+(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t)|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad=\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U\chi|x-\xi|^{2}dx+\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}(x-\xi,t)|x-\xi|^{2}dx+(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t)|x-\xi|^{2}dx.

Replacing this in (
5.21
) we obtain (
5.20
).
∎

In the definition (
3.12
) of
u_{1}
we will stress the dependence on the parameters by writing
\mathbf{p}=(\lambda,\alpha,\xi)
and
u_{1}=u_{1}(\mathbf{p})
.
At this point we would like to construct
\lambda_{0}
and
\alpha_{0}
so that setting
\mathbf{p}_{0}=(\lambda_{0},\alpha_{0},0)
we have

(5.22)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0})dx

\displaystyle=8\pi,

(5.23)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0}))|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=O\Bigl(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3}{2}+\sigma}}\Bigr),

for some
\sigma>0
. The reason for allowing in (
5.23
) an error is that it is difficult to solve with right hand side equal to 0 and a remainder of size
O(t^{-\frac{3}{2}-\sigma})
with
\sigma>0
is sufficiently small to proceed with the rest of the construction.

Assuming that (
5.22
) holds,
we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1})|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}(x-\xi,t;\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}\,dx\cdot\dot{\xi}-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U\chi|x-\xi|^{2}dx-(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t;\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}dx.

It turns out that the main terms in the expression for
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1})|x-\xi|^{2}dx
are the first two.
So the equation

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p_{0}}))|x-\xi|^{2}dx=0

is at main order given by

\displaystyle 4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=0.

It will be shown later that

(5.24)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}|x-\xi|^{2}dx=-64\pi\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}+O\Big(\frac{\lambda^{4}}{t^{2}}\Big),

see Lemma
7.5
, where
\Upsilon
is given in (
2.7
), so that the equation we want to solve becomes at main order,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx+16\pi\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}=0.

In
§
7
we will show that

(5.25)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx=-4\pi\int_{t/2}^{t-\lambda^{2}}\frac{\lambda\dot{\lambda}}{t-s}ds-2\pi\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}-16\pi\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}+O\Big(\frac{\lambda^{4}\log\log t}{t}\Big)

see Corollary
7.1
.
Using (
5.25
) we see that

(5.26)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx+16\pi\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}=-4\pi\Bigl[\int_{t/2}^{t-\lambda^{2}}\frac{\lambda\dot{\lambda}}{t-s}ds+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2t}\Bigr]+O\Big(\frac{\lambda^{4}\log\log t}{t}\Big)

so that the equation for
\lambda
is at main order

\displaystyle\int_{t/2}^{t-\lambda^{2}}\frac{\lambda\dot{\lambda}}{t-s}ds+\frac{\lambda^{2}}{2t}=0.

One can check that
\lambda^{*}(t)=\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{\log t}}
, where
c_{0}>0
is an arbitrary constant, is an approximate solution. Indeed

\displaystyle\int_{t/2}^{t-(\lambda^{*})^{2}}\frac{\lambda^{*}(s)\dot{\lambda}^{*}(s)}{t-s}ds+\frac{(\lambda^{*})^{2}}{2t}

\displaystyle\approx\lambda^{*}(t)\dot{\lambda}^{*}(t)\int_{t/2}^{t-(\lambda^{*})^{2}}\frac{ds}{t-s}+\frac{\lambda^{*}(t)^{2}}{2t}

\displaystyle\approx\lambda^{*}(t)\dot{\lambda}^{*}(t)\log t+\frac{\lambda^{*}(t)^{2}}{2t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\bigl[\lambda^{*}(t)^{2}\log t\Bigr]=0.

The error left out in the approximation (
5.26
) is too big. We give next a result that shows that for an appropriate modification of
\lambda^{*}
we can achieve a smaller error.
Let us write
\tilde{E}(\lambda)
the expression defined in (
3.10
) with the explicit dependence on
\lambda
.

Proposition 5.1
.

Let
c_{0}>0
be fixed.
For
t_{0}>0
sufficiently large there exists
\lambda_{0}:[\frac{t_{0}}{2},\infty)\to(0,\infty)
such that

(5.27)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda_{0}}dx-\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}(\lambda_{0})|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=O\Bigl(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3}{2}+\sigma}}\Bigr),\quad t>t_{0},

for some
\sigma>0
. Moreover, for arbitrarily
\varepsilon>0
small,
\lambda_{0}
has the expansion

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{\log t}}+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{(\log t)^{\frac{3}{2}-\varepsilon}}\Bigr),

\displaystyle\dot{\lambda}_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=-\frac{c_{0}}{2t(\log t)^{3/2}}+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{t(\log t)^{\frac{5}{2}-\varepsilon}}\Bigr),

\displaystyle|\ddot{\lambda}_{0}(t)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{t^{2}(\log t)^{3/2}},

as
t\to\infty
.

We will prove this result in §
7.1
.

Once
\lambda_{0}
is constructed in Proposition
5.1
we choose
\alpha_{0}
so that (
5.22
) holds, by imposing

(5.28)

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}(t)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(y)\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{0}(t)y}{\sqrt{t}}\Bigr)\,dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda_{0}}(x,t)\,dx=8\pi,\quad t>t_{0}.

We note that by (
2.6
), (
5.27
) and (
5.24
) we get

\displaystyle\alpha_{0}(t)

\displaystyle=O\Bigl(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3}{2}+\sigma}}\Bigr)

as
t\to\infty
.
A byproduct of the proof of Proposition
5.1
is that

(5.29)

\displaystyle\left|\frac{d}{dt}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda_{0}}dx\right|\leq\frac{C}{t^{2}},

and from this and (
5.28
) we get

(5.30)

\displaystyle|\dot{\alpha}_{0}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{2}}.

As a corollary of Proposition
5.1
we get:

Corollary 5.1
.

Let
\textbf{p}_{0}=(\lambda_{0},\alpha_{0},0)

with

\alpha_{0}
defined by (
5.22
) and
\lambda_{0}
be given by Proposition
5.1
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}))|x-\xi|^{2}dx=O\Bigl(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3}{2}+\sigma}}\Bigr),

for some
\sigma>0
.

Proof.

Using Lemma
5.2
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1})|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda_{0}}dx-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}(x-\xi,t;\lambda_{0})|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\dot{\alpha}_{0}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U\chi|x-\xi|^{2}dx-(1-\alpha_{0})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t;\lambda_{0})|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=O\Bigl(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3}{2}+\sigma}}\Bigr),

for some
\sigma>0
, since
\dot{\alpha}_{0}(t)=O(\frac{1}{t^{2}\log t})
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t;\lambda_{0})|x-\xi|^{2}dx=O\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}\Bigr)

by (
5.24
) and a direct estimate for the remaining terms in
E
(c.f. (
3.9
)).
∎

5.2.
A further improvement of the approximation

We introduce a correction
\phi_{0}^{i}(y)
,
y=\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}
in the inner approximation to eliminate the radial part of
S(u_{1}(\textbf{p}))
(defined in (
4.2
)), which we define as

\displaystyle S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}))

\displaystyle=-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}U(y)\chi+(\alpha-1)\frac{\dot{\lambda}}{\lambda^{3}}Z_{0}\chi+\frac{(\alpha-1)}{2t}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}\cdot\frac{x-\xi}{\sqrt{t}}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{2(\alpha-1)}{\lambda^{3}t^{1/2}}\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{y}U+\frac{(\alpha-1)}{t}\Delta\chi_{0}\frac{1}{\lambda^{2}}U-\frac{\alpha^{2}-1}{\lambda^{3}\sqrt{t}}U\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0}-\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{3}\sqrt{t}}U\nabla_{z}\chi_{0}\cdot\nabla_{y}\mathcal{R}

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{\alpha(\alpha-1)\chi}{\lambda^{4}}\nabla_{y}\cdot(U\nabla_{y}\Gamma_{0})+\frac{\alpha^{2}\chi(1-\chi)}{\lambda^{4}}U^{2}-\frac{\alpha\chi}{\lambda^{4}}\nabla_{y}U\cdot\nabla_{y}\mathcal{R}.

(5.31)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{4}{r}\partial_{r}\varphi_{\lambda}-\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla v_{0})-\nabla\cdot(u_{0}\nabla\psi_{\lambda})-\nabla\cdot(\varphi_{\lambda}\nabla\psi_{\lambda}).

With this definition

\displaystyle S(u_{1})=S_{0}(u_{1})+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{3}}\dot{\xi}\cdot{\nabla}_{y}U(y)\,\chi+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{t}}U(y)\dot{\xi}\cdot\nabla\chi_{0},

and the terms not in
S(u_{1})
correspond to
\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{3}}\dot{\xi}\cdot{\nabla}_{y}U(y)\,\chi+\frac{\alpha}{\lambda^{2}\sqrt{t}}U(y)
which are in mode 1.

Then we want
\phi_{0}^{i}
to be an appropriate solution to the equation

(5.32)

\displaystyle L[\phi^{i}_{0}]+\lambda^{4}S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}))(x,t)=c_{0}(t)W_{2}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad x=\xi+\lambda y,

where
L
is the linear operator (
5.4
),

t>t_{0}
is regarded as a parameter,

W_{2}(y)
is a fixed smooth radial function with compact support, and

(5.33)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W_{2}(y)dy=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W_{2}(y)|y|^{2}dy=1.

By Lemma
5.2
and Proposition
5.1
, the choice
\textbf{p}=\textbf{p}_{0}
is so that (
5.22
), (
5.23
) hold. Since the difference between
S(u_{1})
and
S_{0}(u_{1})
contains terms in mode 1 only, we get from Corollary
5.1

(5.34)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\lambda^{4}S_{0}(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0}))|y|^{2}dy

\displaystyle=O\Bigl(\frac{1}{t^{\frac{3}{2}+\sigma}}\Bigr).

In (
5.32
) we select
c_{0}(t)
such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}[\lambda^{4}S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}))+c_{0}(t)W_{2}]|y|^{2}dy=0,\quad t>t_{0}

and thanks to (
5.34
) we have

(5.35)

\displaystyle|c_{0}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{\frac{3}{2}+\sigma}},\quad t>t_{0}.

Note that we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}))dx=0,

which follows from the constant mass in time of
u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0})
in (
5.22
) and the form of the operator
S_{0}
(
5.31
).

We let
\phi^{i}_{0}
be the solution
to (
5.32
) constructed in Lemma
5.1
.
By (
5.15
) and (
4.5
)

(5.36)

\displaystyle|\phi^{i}_{0}(y,t)|\leq\frac{C}{t}\frac{\log(1+|y|)}{1+|y|^{4}},

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{i}_{0}(y,t)dy=0,\quad t>t_{0}.

5.3.
Reformulation of the system

In the outer problem (
5.9
) we would like to separate the effect of the initial condition from the coupling
G(\phi^{i},\varphi^{o},\mathbf{p})
.

We take the initial condition in (
5.9
) to be

\varphi^{o}(\cdot,t_{0})=\varphi_{0}^{*},

and let
\varphi^{*}(x,t)
denote the solution of

(5.37)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\varphi^{*}&=\Delta\varphi^{*}-\nabla_{x}\Gamma_{0}\Big(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}\Big)\cdot\nabla\varphi^{*}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty)\\
\varphi^{*}(\cdot,t_{0})&=\varphi^{*}_{0}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

The initial condition
\varphi^{*}_{0}(x)
will be later used to prove the stability claimed in Theorem
1.1
. The topology for
\varphi_{0}^{*}
will be specified later on.

Note that
\nabla_{x}\Gamma_{0}(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda})=-4\frac{x-\xi}{|x-\xi|^{2}+\lambda^{2}}
so that
\varphi^{*}
is a function of the parameters
\lambda,\xi
. Therefore we will write
\varphi^{*}(x,t;\textbf{p})
when convenient.

We decompose

(5.38)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \phi^{i}&=\phi^{i}_{0}+\phi\\
\varphi^{o}&=\varphi^{*}+\varphi\\
\textbf{p}&=\textbf{p}_{0}+\textbf{p}_{1}\end{aligned}\right.

where

\displaystyle\textbf{p}_{0}=(\lambda_{0},\alpha_{0},0),\quad\textbf{p}_{1}=(\lambda_{1},\alpha_{1},\xi_{1}),

with
\lambda_{0}
the function constructed in Proposition
5.1
and
\alpha_{0}
chosen so that (
5.22
) holds.

We substitute the expressions for
\phi^{i}
,
\varphi^{o}
and
p
in
(
5.38
) into the equations (
5.5
), (
5.9
),
and are led to the following problem for
\phi
,
\varphi

(5.39)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\phi&=L[\phi]+B_{0}[\phi]+E_{2}\tilde{\chi}_{2}+F_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi^{*}_{0})\tilde{\chi}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty)\\
\phi(\cdot,t_{0})&=\phi_{0}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\end{aligned}\right.

(5.40)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\varphi&=\Delta\varphi-\nabla_{x}\Gamma_{0}(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda})\cdot\nabla\varphi+G_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\mathbf{p}_{1},\varphi^{*}_{0})\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty)\\
\varphi(\cdot,t_{0})&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\tilde{\chi}
is defined in (
5.8
),

\displaystyle E_{2}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}\phi^{i}_{0}+B_{0}[\phi^{i}_{0}]+c_{0}(t)W_{2}

\displaystyle F_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi^{*}_{0})

\displaystyle=F(\phi^{i}_{0}+\phi,\varphi^{*}+\varphi,\textbf{p}_{0}+\textbf{p}_{1})+\lambda^{4}[S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}+\textbf{p}_{1}))-S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}))]

(5.41)

\displaystyle\quad+\lambda\alpha\dot{\xi}_{1}\cdot{\nabla}_{y}U(y)\,\chi+\frac{\alpha\lambda^{2}}{\sqrt{t}}U(y)\dot{\xi}_{1}\cdot\nabla\chi_{0}

(5.42)

\displaystyle G_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\mathbf{p}_{1},\varphi^{*}_{0})

\displaystyle=G(\phi^{i}_{0}+\phi,\varphi^{*}+\varphi,\textbf{p}_{0}+\textbf{p}_{1})+\lambda^{-4}E_{2}(1-\tilde{\chi}_{2})\chi

\displaystyle\tilde{\chi}_{2}(x,t)=\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi}{t^{\frac{1}{2}-\delta}}\Bigr),

\delta>0
is a small constant to be fixed later on, and
\chi_{0}
is as in (
2.5
).
We recall that
F
and
G
are defined in (
5.6
) and (
5.10
).
The expressions for
F_{2}
and
G_{2}
depend on the initial condition
\varphi^{*}_{0}
through
\varphi^{*}
(
5.37
) and
\phi_{0}
. The role of
\phi_{0}
will be clarified later on.

By the estimate for
\ddot{\lambda}_{0}
in Proposition
5.1
and (
5.35
) we get

(5.43)

\displaystyle|E_{2}(y,t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{2}(\log t)^{2}}\frac{\log(1+|y|)}{1+|y|^{4}}+\frac{C}{t^{\frac{3}{2}+\sigma}}|W_{2}(y)|,\quad|y|\leq C\sqrt{t\log t}.

The reason that we introduce the cut-off
\tilde{\chi}_{2}
is to achieve

\displaystyle|E_{2}\tilde{\chi}_{2}(y,t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{\nu}(1+|y|)^{6+\sigma}},

if
\nu<1+2\delta-\frac{\sigma}{2}
. We will choose
\delta
and
\sigma
positive small numbers such that
2\delta-\frac{\sigma}{2}>0
so that we can find
1<\nu<1+2\delta-\frac{\sigma}{2}
.

5.4.
Splitting the inner solution
\phi

We perform one more change in the formulation (
5.39
), (
5.40
), which consists in decomposing

\phi=\phi_{1}+\phi_{2}.

The function
\phi_{1}
will solve an equation with part of the right hand side of (
5.39
), which will be projected so that it satisfies the zero second moment condition.

For any
h(y,t)
with sufficient spatial decay we define

(5.44)

\displaystyle m_{0}[h](t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,t)dy,\quad m_{2}[h](t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,t)|y|^{2}dy,

and

\displaystyle m_{1,j}[h](t)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,t)y_{j}dy,\quad j=1,2,

which denote the mass, second moment and center of mass of
h
.

Let
W_{0}\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be radial with compact support such that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W_{0}dy=1,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W_{0}|y|^{2}dy=0.

Let
W_{1,j}
,
j=1,2
be a smooth functions with compact support and with the form
W_{1,j}(y)=\tilde{W}(|y|)y_{j}
so that

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}W_{1,j}(y)y_{j}=1.

We recall that
W_{2}
defined in (
5.33
).

Then,
h-m_{0}[h]W_{0}
has zero mass,
h-m_{2}[h]W_{2}
has zero second moment, and
h-m_{1,1}[h]W_{1,1}-m_{1,2}[h]W_{1,2}
has zero center of mass.

We modify of the operator
B_{0}
appearing in (
5.39
), and defined in (
5.7
).
The idea is to work with a variant of it, which coincides with it for radial functions, but for functions without radial part it is cutoff outside the region
|y|\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{t}}{\lambda}
.
More precisely, we decompose
\phi
in a radial part
[\phi]_{rad}
defined
by

(5.45)

\displaystyle[\phi]_{rad}(\rho,t)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\phi(\rho e^{i\theta},t)d\theta

and a term with no radial mode
\phi_{1}=\phi-[\phi]_{rad}
.
We note that the other linear terms in the equation behave well with this decomposition.
Then we define

(5.46)

\displaystyle B[\phi]=\lambda\dot{\lambda}(2[\phi]_{rad}+y\cdot\nabla[\phi]_{rad})+\lambda\dot{\lambda}(2\phi_{1}+y\cdot\nabla\phi_{1})\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda y}{5\sqrt{t}}\Bigr)

where
\chi_{0}
is a smooth cut-off in
\mathbb{R}
with
\chi_{0}(s)=1
for
s\leq 1
and
\chi_{0}(s)=1
for
s\geq 2
.

With these definitions we introduce the following system for
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
,
\varphi
,
\textbf{p}_{1}
,

(5.47)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\phi_{1}&=L[\phi_{1}]+B[\phi_{1}]+F_{3}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})\\
&\quad-m_{0}[F_{3}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})]W_{0}-m_{2}[F_{3}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})]W_{2}\\
&\quad-m_{1,1}[F_{3}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})]W_{1,1}-m_{1,2}[F_{3}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})]W_{1,2}\\
&\qquad\qquad\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty)\\
\phi_{1}(\cdot,t_{0})&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

(5.48)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\phi_{2}&=L[\phi_{2}]+B[\phi_{2}]+m_{2}[F_{3}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})]W_{2}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty)\\
\phi_{2}(\cdot,t_{0})&=\phi_{0}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

(5.49)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{t}\varphi&=\Delta\varphi-\nabla\Gamma_{0}\cdot\nabla\varphi+G_{2}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\varphi,\mathbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty)\\
\varphi(\cdot,t_{0})&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

where

(5.50)

\displaystyle F_{3}(\phi,\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})=E_{2}\tilde{\chi}_{2}+F_{2}(\phi,\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})\tilde{\chi},

A solution
\phi_{1}
,
\phi_{2}
,
\varphi
to (
5.47
), (
5.48
) and (
5.49
) gives a solution to the system (
5.39
), (
5.40
) provided
\textbf{p}_{1}
is such that the following equations are satisfied

(5.51)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} 0&=m_{0}[F_{3}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})]\\
0&=m_{1,j}[F_{3}(\phi_{1}+\phi_{2},\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})],\quad j=1,2.\end{aligned}\right.

5.5.
Mass and second moment

In this section we derive some formulas for the mass and second moment appearing in the right hand side of (
5.47
).

In the computation of
m_{0}[F_{3}(\phi,\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})]
and
m_{2}[F_{3}(\phi,\varphi,\textbf{p}_{1},\varphi_{0}^{*})]
, the following formulas will be useful.

Lemma 5.3
.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}))dx

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}dx-\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}\Bigl\{8\pi\alpha\Bigl[1+2\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr]+\alpha e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx\Bigr\}

and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}))-S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0})))dx

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}\Bigl\{\alpha_{1}\Bigl[8\pi\Bigl(1+2\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)+e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)\Bigr]+16\pi\alpha_{0}\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha_{0}\Bigl(e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)-e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}\Bigr)\Bigr)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\varphi_{\lambda}-\varphi_{\lambda_{0}})dx\Bigr\},

where
e_{1}(s)
is defined by

(5.52)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}dx=8\pi\alpha\Bigl[1+2\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr]+\alpha e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr).

Recall that
\Upsilon
is given in (
2.7
) and note that

\displaystyle e_{1}(s)=O(s^{2}),\quad\text{as }s\to 0.

Proof.

For this we recall that (c.f. (
2.8
))

\displaystyle S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}))=-\partial_{t}u_{0}-\partial_{t}\varphi_{\lambda}+\mathcal{E}(u_{0}+\varphi_{\lambda}),

so

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}))dx

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}dx-\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}\Bigl\{8\pi\alpha\Bigl[1+2\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr]+\alpha e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx\Bigr\}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}))-S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0})))dx

\displaystyle=-\partial_{t}\Bigl\{\alpha_{1}\Bigl[8\pi\Bigl(1+2\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)+e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)\Bigr]+16\pi\alpha_{0}\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}-\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}

\displaystyle\quad+\alpha_{0}\Bigl(e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)-e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}\Bigr)\Bigr)+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\varphi_{\lambda}-\varphi_{\lambda_{0}})dx\Bigr\}.

∎

Lemma 5.4
.

We have

\displaystyle\lambda^{4}m_{2}

\displaystyle[S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}+\textbf{p}_{1}))-S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}))]

\displaystyle=-32\pi\alpha_{1}-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda})\chi_{0}(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda})|x-\xi|^{2}\,dx+\frac{\dot{\alpha}_{0}}{\lambda_{0}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(\frac{x}{\lambda_{0}})\chi(\frac{x}{\lambda_{0}})|x|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-4\Bigl[\alpha e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)-\alpha_{0}e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}\Bigr)\Bigr]-\Bigl[\alpha e_{2}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)-\alpha_{0}e_{2}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad-\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\varphi_{\lambda}-\varphi_{\lambda_{0}})\,dx\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t,\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}\,dx+(1-\alpha_{0})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x,t,\lambda_{0})|x|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-|\xi|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0}))dx.

Proof.

We have defined the second moment
m_{2}
(
5.44
) integrating with respect to
y
. Note that

\lambda^{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(y)|y|^{2}dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f\Bigl(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda}\Bigr)|x-\xi|^{2}dx,

and therefore

\displaystyle\lambda^{4}m_{2}[S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}+\textbf{p}_{1}))-S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}))]

\displaystyle=\lambda^{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}+\textbf{p}_{1}))(\xi+\lambda y)|y|^{2}dy

\displaystyle\quad-\lambda^{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}))(\xi+\lambda y)|y|^{2}dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}+\textbf{p}_{1}))(x)|x-\xi|^{2}dy

\displaystyle\quad-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\textbf{p}_{0}))(x)|x-\xi|^{2}dy.

We have by Lemma
5.2
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1})|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}dx-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}(x-\xi,t;\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}\,dx\cdot\dot{\xi}-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U\chi|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad-(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t;\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}dx

(5.53)

\displaystyle\quad+4\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}\Bigr)\Bigl(1-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi_{\lambda}\Bigr).

where
E
,
\tilde{E}
are defined in (
3.9
), (
3.10
).
Let

\displaystyle m=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(u_{0}+\varphi_{\lambda})dx,\quad\delta m=m-8\pi.

Since

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(u_{0}+\varphi_{\lambda_{0}})dx=8\pi,

by (
5.22
), we have

\displaystyle\delta m=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\varphi_{\lambda}-\varphi_{\lambda_{0}})\,dx.

Replacing
m
in (
5.53
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}))|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=32\pi-4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}u_{0}dx-\frac{1}{2\pi}(\delta m)^{2}-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}(x-\xi,t;\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}\,dx\cdot\dot{\xi}-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U\chi|x-\xi|^{2}dx

(5.54)

\displaystyle\quad-(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t;\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}dx.

Also under (
4.1
) we have by
(
5.24
):

(5.55)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{E}|x-\xi|^{2}dx=-64\pi\Upsilon\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}+e_{2}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr),

where

\displaystyle e_{2}(s)=O(s^{2}),\quad\text{as }s\to 0.

Combining (
5.54
), (
5.52
) and (
5.55
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}))|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=32\pi(1-\alpha)-\frac{1}{2\pi}(\delta m)^{2}-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U\chi|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\varphi_{\lambda}\,dx\cdot\dot{\xi}-(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t;\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad-4\alpha e_{1}\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Big)-\alpha e_{2}\Big(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Big).

We can apply this formula to
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}_{0}
and get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0}))|x|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=32\pi(1-\alpha_{0})-\frac{\dot{\alpha}_{0}}{\lambda_{0}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(\frac{x}{\lambda_{0}})\chi|x|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad-(1-\alpha_{0})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x,t;\lambda_{0})|x|^{2}dx

\displaystyle\quad-4\alpha_{0}e_{1}\Big(\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}\Big)-\alpha_{0}e_{2}\Big(\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}\Big).

Note that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0}))|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0}))|x|^{2}dx+|\xi|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0}))dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0})|x|^{2}dx+|\xi|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0})dx

because

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S_{0}(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0})x_{j}dx=0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}

\displaystyle[S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}))-S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0}))]|x-\xi|^{2}dx

\displaystyle=-32\pi\alpha_{1}-\frac{\dot{\alpha}}{\lambda^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda})\chi_{0}(\frac{x-\xi}{\lambda})|x-\xi|^{2}\,dx+\frac{\dot{\alpha}_{0}}{\lambda_{0}^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(\frac{x}{\lambda_{0}})\chi(\frac{x}{\lambda_{0}})|x|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-4\Bigl[\alpha e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)-\alpha_{0}e_{1}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}\Bigr)\Bigr]-\Bigl[\alpha e_{2}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda^{2}}{t}\Bigr)-\alpha_{0}e_{2}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda_{0}^{2}}{t}\Bigr)\Bigr]

\displaystyle\quad-\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\varphi_{\lambda}-\varphi_{\lambda_{0}})\,dx\Bigr)^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-(1-\alpha)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x-\xi,t,\lambda)|x-\xi|^{2}\,dx+(1-\alpha_{0})\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}E(x,t,\lambda_{0})|x|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\quad-|\xi|^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}S(u_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{0})dx.

∎

6.
Proof of Theorem
1.1

Next we define norms, which are suitably adapted to the terms in the inner linear problems (
5.47
), (
5.48
).
Let us write the linearized versions of these problems as

(6.1)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\phi&=L[\phi]+B[\phi]+h(y,t)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty),\\
\phi(\cdot,t_{0})&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

Given positive numbers
\nu
,
p
,
\epsilon
and
m\in\mathbb{R}
, we let

(6.2)

\displaystyle\|h\|_{0,\nu,m,p,\epsilon}=

\displaystyle\inf K\quad\text{such that }

\displaystyle|h(y,t)|\leq\frac{K}{t^{\nu}(\log t)^{m}}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{p}}\begin{cases}\displaystyle 1&|y|\leq\sqrt{t\log t},\\
\displaystyle\frac{(t\log t)^{\epsilon/2}}{|y|^{\epsilon}}&|y|\geq\sqrt{t\log t}.\end{cases}

We also defie

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{1,\nu,m,p,\epsilon}=

\displaystyle\inf K\quad\text{such that }

\displaystyle|\phi(y,t)|+(1+|y|)|\nabla_{y}\phi(y,t)|\leq\frac{K}{t^{\nu}(\log t)^{m}}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{p}}\begin{cases}\displaystyle 1&|y|\leq\sqrt{t\log t},\\
\displaystyle\frac{(t\log t)^{\epsilon/2}}{|y|^{\epsilon}}&|y|\geq\sqrt{t\log t}.\end{cases}

We develop a solvability theory of problem (
6.1
) that involves uniform space-time bounds in terms of the above norms.
We will establish two results: one in which
the solution “loses” one power of
t
on bounded sets with respect to the time-decay of
h
, under radial symmetry and the condition of spatial average 0 at all times. Our second result states that for a general
h

this loss is only
t^{\frac{1}{2}}
if in addition the center of mass and second-moment of
h
are zero at all times.

For the first result we introduce a parameter in the problem in order to get a fast decay of the solution:

(6.3)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\phi&=L[\phi]+B[\phi]+h(y,t)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty),\\
\phi(\cdot,t_{0})&=c_{1}\tilde{Z}_{0}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\tilde{Z}_{0}
is defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{0}(\rho)

\displaystyle=(Z_{0}(\rho)-m_{Z_{0}}U)\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{3\lambda(t_{0})\sqrt{t_{0}}}\Bigr),

where
m_{Z_{0}}
is such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{Z}_{0}=0.

Proposition 6.1
.

Assume (
4.1
).
Let
\sigma>0
,
\epsilon>0
with
\sigma+\epsilon<2
and
1<\nu<\frac{7}{4}
. Let
0<q<1
.
Then there exists a number
C>0
such that for
t_{0}
sufficiently large and all radially symmetric
h=h(|y|,t)
with
\|h\|_{0,\nu,m,6+\sigma,\epsilon}<\infty
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,t)dy

\displaystyle=0,\quad\text{for all }t>t_{0},

there exists
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and solution
\phi(y,t)=\mathcal{T}^{i,2}_{\textbf{p}}[h]
of problem
(
6.3
)
that defines a linear operator of
h
and
satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{1,\nu-1,m+q-1,4,2+\sigma+\epsilon}\leq\frac{C}{(\log t_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{0,\nu,m,6+\sigma,\epsilon}.

Moreover
c_{1}
is a linear operator of
