\displaystyle=-\eta(t)I_{r}[\dot{\mu}_{1}]+\frac{1}{8\pi((1-\vartheta)\log(t)-2\log(\lambda^{*}(t)))}\eta(t)\bigl[e_{1}(t)+F[\mu_{1}](t)\bigr],\ t>\frac{t_{0}}{2},

where

\displaystyle I_{r}[\dot{\mu}_{1}]=\frac{1}{((1-\vartheta)\log(t)-2\log(\lambda^{*}(t))}\int_{t/2}^{t-t^{1-\vartheta}}\frac{\dot{\mu}_{1}(s)}{t-s}ds.

We directly check that

\displaystyle\|I_{r}[\dot{\mu}]\|_{\gamma,m}\leq\frac{\vartheta}{1-\vartheta}\|\dot{\mu}_{1}\|_{\gamma,m}.

Let
X
be the space
X=\{\mu_{1}\in C^{1}([\frac{t_{0}}{2},\infty))\,|\,\lim_{t\to\infty}\mu_{1}(t)=0\}
with the norm
\|\mu_{1}\|_{X}=\|\mu_{1}\|_{*,1,3-\varepsilon}
, where
0<\varepsilon<1
.
It follows that if
\vartheta<\frac{1}{2}
the equation (
7.25
) has a unique solution
\mu_{1}
in the ball
\overline{B}_{1}(0)
of
X
.

Therefore we have found
\mu_{1}
with

\|\mu_{1}\|_{*,1,3-\varepsilon}\leq 1

so that
\mu=\mu^{*}+\mu_{1}
satisfies

(7.26)

\displaystyle\tilde{I}[\mu]=-8\pi\eta(t)\int_{t-t^{1-\vartheta}}^{t-\lambda^{*}(t)^{2}}\frac{\dot{\mu}_{1}(t)-\dot{\mu}_{1}(s)}{t-s}ds.

To estimate this remainder we then need a bound for
\ddot{\mu}
.
Differentiating with respect to
t
in the decompositions used in Lemmas
7.1
,
7.4
,
7.5

we obtain

\displaystyle|\dot{e}_{1}(t)|\leq C\frac{\log(\log t)}{t^{2}(\log t)^{2}},\quad t>\frac{t_{0}}{2}.

Differentiating in
t
equation (
7.25
) and using the contraction mapping principle we get that for any
\varepsilon>0
small

\displaystyle|\ddot{\mu}_{1}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{2-\varepsilon}}.

Using this we find that the remainder (
7.26
) has the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{t-t^{1-\vartheta}}^{t-\lambda^{*}(t)^{2}}\frac{\dot{\mu}(t)-\dot{\mu}(s)}{t-s}ds\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{t^{1+\vartheta-\varepsilon}},\quad t>\frac{t_{0}}{2},

where
\mu=\mu^{*}+\mu_{1}
.

Next we introduce another correction
\mu_{2}
to improve the decay of the remainder.
We consider
\mu=\mu^{*}+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
and we consider the following equation for
\mu_{2}
:

\displaystyle\ell[\mu^{*}+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}]+N[\mu^{*}+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}]+R[\mu^{*}+\mu_{1}]=0,\quad t>\frac{t_{0}}{2}.

Similarly as before, this equation can be written as

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-8\pi\dot{\mu}_{2}((1-\vartheta)\log(t)-2\log(\lambda^{*}(t)))-8\pi\eta(t)\int_{t/2}^{t-t^{1-\vartheta}}\frac{\dot{\mu}_{2}(s)}{t-s}ds

(7.27)

\displaystyle\quad+\eta(t)e_{2}(t)+\eta(t)F_{2}[\mu_{2}](t),\quad t>\frac{t_{0}}{2},

where
F_{2}
satisfies the same estimate (
7.23
) (
7.24
), and
e_{2}
has the estimate

\displaystyle|e_{2}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{1+\vartheta-\varepsilon}},\quad t>\frac{t_{0}}{2}.

Using again the contraction mapping principle we find a solution
\mu_{2}
of (
7.27
) with
\|\mu_{2}\|_{*,1+\vartheta-\varepsilon,1}\leq 1
.
Then for
\mu=\mu^{*}+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}

\displaystyle\tilde{I}[\mu](t)=-8\pi\eta(t)\int_{t-t^{1-\vartheta}}^{t-\lambda^{*}(t)^{2}}\frac{\dot{\mu}_{2}(t)-\dot{\mu}_{2}(s)}{t-s}ds.

To estimate this remainder we need the following bound for
\ddot{\mu}_{2}

(7.28)

\displaystyle|\ddot{\mu}_{2}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{2+\vartheta-\varepsilon}}

which is obtained from an estimate for
\dot{e}_{2}
, differentiating with respect to
t
equation (
7.27
). The estimate for
\dot{e}_{2}
is obtained from an analogous estimate for
\frac{d^{3}\mu_{1}}{dt^{3}}
.

From (
7.28
) we find

\displaystyle|\tilde{I}[\mu](t)|\leq\frac{C}{t^{1+2\vartheta-\varepsilon}}\quad t>\frac{t_{0}}{2},

where we recall that
0<\vartheta<\frac{1}{2}
is arbitrary.

Thus letting
\lambda_{0}=\sqrt{\mu}
,
\mu=\mu^{*}+\mu_{1}+\mu_{2}
we obtain

\displaystyle|I[\lambda_{0}]|\leq\frac{C}{t^{1+2\vartheta-\varepsilon}}\quad t>t_{0}.

Choosing
\vartheta>\frac{1}{4}
and
\varepsilon>0
small, we obtain the properties stated in Proposition
5.1
.

∎

8.
Inner linear theory

In this section we consider the problem

(8.1)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \lambda^{2}\partial_{t}\phi&=L[\phi]+B[\phi]+h(y,t)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(t_{0},\infty)\\
\phi(\cdot,t_{0})&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

that appears in the inner equations (
5.47
) and (
5.48
),
where, we recall

\displaystyle L[\phi]

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\Bigl[U\nabla\Bigl(\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi\Bigr)\Bigr],

(8.2)

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi(y,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\log\Bigl(\frac{1}{|y-z|}\Bigr)\phi(z,t)dz.

Slightly more general than the operator
B
defined in (
5.46
) we will consider

\displaystyle B[\phi]=\zeta_{1}(t)[\phi]_{rad}+\zeta_{2}(t)y\cdot\nabla[\phi]_{rad}+(\zeta_{1}(t)\phi_{1}+\zeta_{2}(t)y\cdot\nabla\phi_{1})\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\lambda y}{5\sqrt{t}}\Bigr)

where
[\phi]_{rad}
is the radial part of
\phi
(defined in (
5.45
)) and
\phi_{1}=\phi-[\phi]_{rad}
, and
where
\chi_{0}
is the smooth cut-off function defined in (
2.5
).
In the sequel we will keep the same notation for
B
.

In what follows we will analyze the linear initial value problem (
8.1
)
where we assume that the functions
\lambda(t)
,
\zeta_{i}(t)
are continuous,
t_{0}>1
and that for some positive numbers
c
,
C
we have

\frac{c}{\sqrt{\log t}}\,\leq\,\lambda(t)\,\leq\,\frac{C}{\sqrt{\log t}}\quad\text{for all }t>t_{0},

\displaystyle|\zeta_{i}(t)|\leq\frac{C}{t\log^{2}t}\quad\text{for all }t>t_{0}.

Next we change the time variable into

\displaystyle\tau=\tau_{0}+\int_{t_{0}}^{t}\frac{1}{\lambda(s)^{2}}ds,

where
\tau_{0}=t_{0}\log t_{0}
.
Then

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{1}t\log t\,\leq\,\tau\,\leq\,\tilde{c}_{2}t\log t

for some
\tilde{c}_{1},\tilde{c}_{2}>0
.
Identifying
\phi(y,t)
and
h(y,t)
with
\phi(y,\tau)
and
h(y,\tau)
we rewrite (
8.1
) as

(8.3)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}\phi&=L[\phi]+B[\phi]+h\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},\infty)\\
\phi(\cdot,\tau_{0})&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

We consider problem (
8.3
) for functions
h(y,\tau)
that have fast decay in space. More precisely, we assume that for all
T>0
there is
C_{T}
such that

\displaystyle|h(y,\tau)|\leq\frac{C_{T}}{1+|y|^{6}}\quad\text{for all }(y,\tau)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},T).

In this case, by a solution
\phi(y,\tau)
of (
8.3
)
we understand a continuous function
\phi(y,\tau)
, of class
C^{1}
in
y
, such that for any
T>\tau_{0}
there exists a
C_{T}>0
with

(8.4)

\displaystyle|\phi(y,\tau)|+(1+|y|)|\nabla_{y}\phi(y,\tau)|\,\leq\,\frac{C_{T}}{1+|y|^{6}}\quad\text{for all }(y,\tau)\in\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},T),

and satisfies the integral equation

(8.5)

\displaystyle\phi(y,\tau)=\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}G(y-z,\tau-s)\,[-\nabla

\displaystyle\phi\nabla\Gamma_{0}-\nabla U\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi

\displaystyle+2U\phi+B[\phi]+h](z,s)\,dzds,

where
(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi
is defined in (
8.2
) and
G(y,\tau)
designates the two-dimensional heat kernel,

\displaystyle G(y,\tau)=\frac{1}{4\pi\tau}e^{-\frac{|y|^{2}}{4\tau}}.

From the formula

\displaystyle\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}h(y)=-\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{y-z}{|y-z|^{2}}h(z)dz

we see that if
|\phi(y)|\leq\frac{C}{1+|y|^{6}}
then

\displaystyle|\nabla(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi(y)|\leq\frac{C}{1+|y|}\|(1+|y|^{6})\phi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Using this estimate,
existence and uniqueness of a solution of (
8.5
) satisfying (
8.4
) are standard. For a short time
T>\tau_{0}
this is established by a contraction mapping argument in an appropriate

L^{\infty}
-weighted space. Then a direct linear continuation procedure applies.

A first natural condition to impose on
h
in (
8.3
) is that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,\tau)dy=0\quad\text{for all }\tau>\tau_{0},

in order to achieve that the solution has also zero mass at all times.

We want to find solutions to (
8.3
) that have fast decay in space and time. For this we need to assume fast space-time decay of the right hand side, which we do by working with the following class of norms.

Given positive numbers
\nu
,
p
,
\epsilon
and
m\in\mathbb{R}
, we let

\|h\|_{\nu,m,p,\epsilon}
denote the least
K\geq 0
such that for all
\tau>\tau_{0}
and for all
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}

(8.6)

\displaystyle|h(y,\tau)|\ \leq\ \,\frac{K}{\tau^{\nu}(\log\tau)^{m}}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{p}}\begin{cases}\displaystyle 1&|y|\leq\sqrt{\tau},\\
\displaystyle\frac{\tau^{\epsilon/2}}{|y|^{\epsilon}}&|y|\geq\sqrt{\tau}.\end{cases}

This is similar to the norm introduced in (
6.2
) but defined using
\tau
instead of
t
.
We will give the results in Sections
9
–
12
using the norm (
8.6
).

Still, fast decay of the right hand side doesn’t imply fast decay of the solution.
For example, consider equation (
8.1
) without the operator
B
, that is,

(8.7)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}\phi&=L[\phi]+h(y,t)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},\infty)\\
\phi(\cdot,\tau_{0})&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

and suppose that
h
has compact support in space and time, and that
\phi
has sufficient space-time decay. Then, multiplying (
8.7
) by
|y|^{2}
and integrating in
\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},\infty)
gives

\displaystyle\int_{\tau_{0}}^{\infty}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,\tau)|y|^{2}dyd\tau=0,

because if
\phi
is a regular function with fast decay, then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}L[\phi]|y|^{2}dy=0,

see Remark
9.2
below.
It is then necessary to impose a condition on
h
, or to adjust a parameter in the problem in order to get a fast decay of the solution.
We develop here the theory by adjusting the parameter
c_{1}
in the equation below

(8.8)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}\phi&=L[\phi]+B[\phi]+h(y,t)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},\infty),\\
\phi(\cdot,t_{0})&=c_{1}\tilde{Z}_{0}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\tilde{Z}_{0}
is defined as

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{0}(\rho)

\displaystyle=(Z_{0}(\rho)-m_{Z_{0}}U)\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{\tau_{0}}}\Bigr),

where
m_{Z_{0}}
is such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{Z}_{0}=0.

Proposition 8.1
.

Let
\sigma>0
,
\epsilon>0
with
\sigma+\epsilon<2
and
1<\nu<\frac{7}{4}
. Let
0<q<1
. Then there exists a number
C>0
such that for
t_{0}
sufficiently large and all radially symmetric
h=h(|y|,\tau)
with
\|h\|_{\nu,m,6+\sigma,\epsilon}<\infty
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,\tau)dy

\displaystyle=0,\quad\text{for all }\tau>\tau_{0},

there exists
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
and solution
\phi(y,\tau)=\mathcal{T}^{i,2}_{\textbf{p}}[h]
of problem
(
8.8
)
that defines a linear operator of
h
and
satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{\nu-1,m+q,4,2+\sigma+\epsilon}\leq\frac{C}{(\log\tau_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{\nu,m,6+\sigma,\epsilon}.

Moreover
c_{1}
is a linear operator of
h
and

\displaystyle|c_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{1}{\tau_{0}^{\nu-1}(\log\tau_{0})^{m+1}}\|h\|_{\nu,m,6+\sigma,\epsilon}.

We have stated this result only in the radial setting, because this is what is needed, but there is a version of it in the non-radial case.

The next result is for the problem

(8.9)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}\phi&=L[\phi]+B[\phi]+h(y,\tau)\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},\infty),\\
\phi(\cdot,\tau_{0})&=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

and holds without the radial symmetry assumption but assuming orthogonality of the right hand side with respect to all elements in the kernel and for all times.

Proposition 8.2
.

Let
0<\sigma<1
,
\epsilon>0
with
\sigma+\epsilon<\frac{3}{2}

and
1<\nu<\min(1+\frac{\epsilon}{2},3-\frac{\sigma}{2},\frac{5}{4})
.
Let
0<q<1
.
Then there is
C
such that for
\tau_{0}
large the following holds.
Suppose that
h
satisfies
\|h\|_{\nu,m,6+\sigma,\epsilon}<\infty
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,\tau)dy

\displaystyle=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,\tau)|y|^{2}dy=0,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,\tau)y_{j}dy

\displaystyle=0,\quad j=1,2,\quad\text{for all }\tau>\tau_{0}.

Then there exists a solution
\phi(y,\tau)=\mathcal{T}^{i,1}_{\textbf{p}}[h]
of problem
(
8.9
)
that defines a linear operator of
h
and
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\phi\|_{\nu-\frac{1}{2},m+\frac{q}{2},4,2+\sigma+\epsilon}\leq C\|h\|_{\nu,m,6+\sigma,\epsilon}.

Propositions
6.1
and
6.2
given in Section
6
are direct corollaries of Propositions
8.1
and
8.2
. The only changes are due to the change in the time variable, because
\tau\sim t\log t
, and the fact that the norms for the solutions in Propositions
6.1
and
6.2
include a gradient term. The estimate for the gradient follows from the weighted
L^{\infty}
estimate, scaling and standard parabolic estimates.

The proofs of Propositions
8.1
and
8.2
are contained in Sections
9
–
12
. They are based on an energy inequality obtained by multiplying the equation by a suitable test function, and using an inequality for a quadratic form.
Section
9
contains some preliminaries on this quadratic form.

In Proposition
10.1
, we obtain an additive decomposition of the solution
\phi(y,\tau)
of (
8.8
) into a part with a relatively slow space decay that loses
\tau^{1/2}
with respect to the time decay of the right hand side, and a term along
Z_{0}(y)
that loses an entire power of
\tau
.
This is the key element for the proof of Proposition
8.1
in Section
10
(p.
10
).

Then the proof of Proposition
8.2

in the radial case uses Proposition
10.1
after formally applying the operator
L^{-1}
to the original equation and performing a
concentration procedure
that improves the space decay of the resulting error. This is done on Section
11
, and we give there a proof of Proposition
8.2
in the case of radial functions.

The proof of Proposition
8.2
in the general case is in Section
12
(p.
12
).. The idea is that the decomposition obtained in Proposition
10.1
for solutions with no radial mode does not contain the term along
Z_{0}
, which allows us to obtain a much better estimate.

9.
Preliminaries for the linear theory

A central ingredient in obtaining good estimates for the linearized parabolic operator associated to the inner problem is the analysis
of the quadratic form

(9.1)

\displaystyle\phi\mapsto\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g\phi,\quad g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi.

This quadratic form arises when considering the linearized Keller-Segel problem (
8.1
).
Indeed,
L[\phi]=\nabla\cdot(U\nabla g)
and it is natural to test the equation (
8.1
) with
g
, since

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}L[\phi]g=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\cdot(U\nabla g)=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g|^{2}.

But from the time derivative we get
\lambda^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\partial_{t}\phi g
, which leads to (
9.1
).

We observe that
g
has degeneracy directions.
Indeed, if
\psi=(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi
then

\Delta\psi+U(y)\psi=-Ug{\quad\hbox{in }}\mathbb{R}^{2}.

The operator
\Delta\psi+U(y)\psi
is classical. It corresponds to linearizing the Liouville equation

\displaystyle\Delta v+e^{v}=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},

around the solution
\Gamma_{0}=\log U
. It is well known that the bounded kernel of this linearization is spanned by the generators of rigid motions, namely dilation and translations of the equation, which are precisely the functions
z_{0},z_{1},z_{2}
defined by

(9.2)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} z_{0}(y)&=\nabla\Gamma_{0}(y)\cdot y+2\\
z_{j}(y)&=\partial_{y_{j}}\Gamma_{0}(y),\quad j=1,2.\end{aligned}\right.

Note that
g
is precisely annihilated at the linear combinations of these functions. In the rest of this section we will state and prove several estimates that take into account this issue, which will be crucial later on.

The quadratic form (
9.1
) can be naturally transformed into a similar one in
S^{2}
by stereographic projection
\Pi:S^{2}\setminus\{(0,0,1)\}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle\Pi(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})=\Bigl(\frac{y_{1}}{1-y_{3}},\frac{y_{2}}{1-y_{3}}\Bigr).

For
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
we write

\tilde{\varphi}=\varphi\circ\Pi,\quad\tilde{\varphi}:S^{2}\setminus\{(0,0,1)\}\to\mathbb{R}.

Then we have the following formulas

\displaystyle\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi U

\displaystyle\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{U}|\nabla_{S^{2}}\tilde{\varphi}|^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi|^{2}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\tilde{U}\Delta_{S^{2}}\tilde{\varphi}

\displaystyle=(\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\varphi)\circ\Pi.

9.1.
The Liouville equation

Here we consider the linearized Liouville equation

(9.3)

\displaystyle\Delta\psi+U\psi+h=0\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.

The stereographic projection transforms
the linearized Liouville equation (
9.3
)
into

(9.4)

\displaystyle\Delta_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}+2\tilde{\psi}+2\tilde{h}=0

in
S^{2}\setminus\{P\}
,
P=(0,0,1)
,
where
\tilde{\psi}=\psi\circ\Pi
,
\tilde{h}=(U^{-1}h)\circ\Pi
.

The functions in (
9.2
) are transformed through the stereographic projection into constant multiples of the coordinate functions

\tilde{z}_{j}(\omega)=c_{j}\omega_{j},\quad j=1,2,\quad\tilde{z}_{0}(\omega)=c_{0}\omega_{3},\quad\omega=(\omega_{1},\omega_{2},\omega_{3})\in S^{2}.

By standard elliptic theory, if
\tilde{h}\in L^{p}(S^{2})
,
p>2
, then exists a solution
\tilde{\psi}_{0}\in W^{2,p}(S^{2})
to (
9.4
) in
S^{2}
if and only if
\tilde{h}
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{h}\tilde{z}_{j}=0,\quad j=1,2,3.

This solution is unique if we normalize it such that

\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}_{0}\tilde{z}_{j}=0,\quad j=1,2,3,

and then satisfies the estimate

\|\tilde{\psi}_{0}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(S^{2})}\leq C\|\tilde{h}\|_{L^{p}(S^{2})}

where
\alpha=1-\frac{2}{p}
.
By subtracting off a suitable linear combination of the functions
\tilde{z}_{j}
,
j=0,1,2
we obtain the unique solution
\tilde{\psi}_{1}
to (
9.4
) in
S^{2}
satisfying

(9.5)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{1}(P)=0,\quad\nabla_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}_{1}(P)=0.

For this solution we also have the estimate

(9.6)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\psi}_{1}\|_{C^{1,\alpha}(S^{2})}\leq C\|\tilde{h}\|_{L^{p}(S^{2})}.

Lemma 9.1
.

Let
0<\sigma<1
. Then there is
C
such that if

\psi
satisfies (
9.3
) and
\psi(y)\to 0
as
|y|\to\infty
with
h
satisfying

\|(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<+\infty
and

(9.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(U\psi+h(y))dy=0,\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(U\psi+h(y))y_{j}\,dy=0,\quad j=1,2,

then

\displaystyle\|(1+|y|)^{1+\sigma}\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\leq\,C\|(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Remark 9.1
.

Let
h:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy
\|(1+|y|)^{2+\sigma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<+\infty
where
0<\sigma<1
.
If

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)dy=0

then

\displaystyle|(-\Delta)^{-1}h(y)|\leq\frac{C}{(1+|y|)^{\sigma}}\|(1+|y|)^{2+\sigma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

If
h:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy
\|(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}<+\infty
where
0<\sigma<1
and in addition to mass zero we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y)y_{j}dy=0,\quad j=1,2,

then

\displaystyle|(-\Delta)^{-1}h(y)|\leq\frac{C}{(1+|y|)^{1+\sigma}}\|(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

The first claim is standard.
For the second, write

(-\Delta)^{-1}h(x)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\log|x|-\log|x-y|+\frac{y\cdot x}{|x|^{2}})h(y)dy

and estimate the integral after splitting it into the regions
|y|<\frac{|x|}{2}
and its complement.

Proof of Lemma
9.1
.

We claim that
\psi=(-\Delta)^{-1}(U\psi+h)
. Indeed the function
\psi-(-\Delta)^{-1}(U\psi+h)
is harmonic in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
and decays to 0 at infinity, and therefore it is equal to 0.
The assumptions (
9.7
) and Remark
9.1
imply that

(9.8)

\displaystyle\|(1+|y|)^{1+\sigma}\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\leq\,C\|(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+C\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Let
\tilde{\psi}=\psi\circ\Pi
, so that it satisfies (
9.4
) in
S^{2}\setminus\{P\}
with
\tilde{h}=(U^{-1}h)\circ\Pi
. Note that
\tilde{h}\in L^{p}(S^{2})
for some
p>2
. More precisely

(9.9)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{h}\|_{L^{p}(S^{2})}\leq C\|(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

with
p<\frac{2}{1-\sigma}
.
The singularity at
P
is removable and thus
\tilde{\psi}
satisfies (
9.4
) in
S^{2}
.
By elliptic regularity
\tilde{\psi}\in C^{1,\alpha}(S^{2})
for some
\alpha>0
.
Since
\psi
decays at infinity,
\tilde{\psi}(P)=0
.
By (
9.8
) we have also
\nabla_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}(P)=0
.

We let
\tilde{\psi}_{1}
denote the solution to (
9.4
) satisfying (
9.5
). The solution to (
9.4
) in
S^{2}
satisfying (
9.5
) is unique, so that we have
\tilde{\psi}=\tilde{\psi}_{1}
and by estimate (
9.6
), (
9.9
) and (
9.8
) we obtain

\displaystyle\|(1+|y|)^{1+\sigma}\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\,\leq\,C\|(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}h\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

∎

9.2.
A quadratic form

Here we discuss properties of the quadratic form (
9.1
).
For this we consider a function
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
with sufficient decay, in the form,

(9.10)

\displaystyle|\phi(y)|\leq\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{2+\sigma}},

with
0<\sigma<1
, and zero mass:

(9.11)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi\,dy

\displaystyle=0.

We recall
g
defined in (
9.1
)
g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi
, and
use the notation

\displaystyle\psi=(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi

so that

\displaystyle-\Delta\psi-U\psi=Ug\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.

We next introduce a normalized version of
g
, namely
g^{\perp}
defined by

\displaystyle g^{\perp}=g+a,

where
a\in\mathbb{R}
is chosen so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g^{\perp}Udy=0.

As shown in Lemma
9.3
below, the quadratic form
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g
is equivalent to
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}
.

It will be convenient to work with functions
\phi^{\perp}
,
\psi^{\perp}
, which are analogues of
\phi
,
\psi
but associated to
g^{\perp}
.
In particular, we want a choice of
\psi^{\perp}
such that

(9.12)

\displaystyle-\Delta\psi^{\perp}-U\psi^{\perp}=Ug^{\perp},\quad\psi^{\perp}(y)\to 0\quad\text{as }|y|\to\infty.

Let
\psi_{0}=1+\frac{1}{2}z_{0}
, where
z_{0}
is defined in (
9.2
), and observe that

-\Delta\psi_{0}-U\psi_{0}=-U,\quad\psi_{0}(y)\to 0\quad\text{as }|y|\to\infty.

Then
\psi^{\perp}
defined by

\psi^{\perp}=\psi-a\Bigl(1+\frac{1}{2}z_{0}\Bigl)=\psi-a\psi_{0},

indeed satisfies (
9.12
).

Define

\phi^{\perp}=U(g^{\perp}+\psi^{\perp}),

and obtain the relations

\displaystyle\phi=\phi^{\perp}+\frac{a}{2}Uz_{0},\quad-\Delta\psi^{\perp}=\phi^{\perp},\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{\perp}=0.

We note that
\phi-\phi^{\perp}=\frac{a}{2}Uz_{0}
is a constant times
Z_{0}=Uz_{0}
, which is in the kernel of the operator
L
.

Lemma 9.2
.

If
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies (
9.10
) and (
9.11
), then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}gUz_{j}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g^{\perp}Uz_{j}=0,\quad j=0,1,2,

where
z_{j}
are the functions defined in (
9.2
).

Proof.

By the definition of
\psi
and from (
9.10
), (
9.11
) we have

\displaystyle|\psi(y)|+(1+|y|)|\nabla\psi(y)|\leq\frac{C}{(1+|y|)^{\sigma}},

and hence also

(9.13)

\displaystyle|\psi^{\perp}(y)|+(1+|y|)|\nabla\psi^{\perp}(y)|\leq\frac{C}{(1+|y|)^{\sigma}}.

We multiply (
9.12
) by
z_{j}
, integrate in the ball
B_{R}(0)
and let
R\to\infty
.
Since
z_{j}
is in the kernel of
\Delta+U
we just have to check that

\displaystyle\int_{\partial B_{R}}\Bigl(\frac{\partial\psi^{\perp}}{\partial\nu}z_{j}-\psi^{\perp}\frac{\partial z_{j}}{\partial\nu}\Bigr)\to 0,\quad\text{as }R\to\infty,

where
\nu
is the exterior normal vector to
\partial B_{R}
.
This follows from (
9.13
), and the explicit bounds

\displaystyle|z_{0}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq C,\quad|z_{j}(y)|\leq\frac{C}{(1+|y|)},\quad j=1,2,

\displaystyle|\nabla z_{j}(y)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{(1+|y|)^{2}}.

∎

A consequence of the previous lemma is the following.

Remark 9.2
.

Suppose that
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies (
9.10
) and (
9.11
).
Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}L[\phi]|y|^{2}dy=0.

Indeed, integrating on
B_{R}
, with the notation
g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi
,

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}L[\phi]|y|^{2}dy

\displaystyle=\int_{B_{R}}\nabla\cdot(U\nabla g)|y|^{2}dy

\displaystyle=-2\int_{B_{R}}U\nabla g\cdot ydy+R^{2}\int_{\partial B_{R}}U\nabla g\cdot\nu dS(y)

\displaystyle=2\int_{B_{R}}gZ_{0}dy-2\int_{\partial B_{R}}Ugy\cdot\nu dy+R^{2}\int_{\partial B_{R}}U\nabla g\cdot\nu dS(y).

By (
9.10
) and (
9.11
),
g(y)=O(|y|^{2-\sigma})
,
\nabla g(y)=O(|y|^{1-\sigma})
as
|y|\to\infty
. Therefore the boundary terms tend to 0 as
R\to\infty
, and we get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}L[\phi]|y|^{2}dy=2\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}gZ_{0}dy=0,

by Lemma
9.2
.

Lemma 9.3
.

There are constants
c_{1}>0
,
c_{2}>0
such that if

\phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\displaystyle|\phi(y)|\leq\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}},\quad 0<\sigma<1

and
(
9.11
), then

(9.14)

\displaystyle c_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}\leq c_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}.

Proof.

By Lemma
9.2

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\phi^{\perp}+\frac{a}{2}Uz_{0})g=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{\perp}(g^{\perp}+a)=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{\perp}g^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp}+\psi^{\perp})g^{\perp}.

We transform
\tilde{g}^{\perp}=g^{\perp}\circ\Pi
,
\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}=\psi^{\perp}\circ\Pi
and write (
9.12
) as

(9.15)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}-2\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}=2\tilde{g}^{\perp},\quad\text{in }S^{2}.

We also get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{2}}[(\tilde{g}^{\perp})^{2}+\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}\tilde{g}^{\perp}].

Multiplying (
9.15
) by
\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}
we find that

\displaystyle\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{g}^{\perp}\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{S^{2}}|\nabla_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}|^{2}-\int_{S^{2}}(\tilde{\psi}^{\perp})^{2}

and hence

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g=\int_{S^{2}}(\tilde{g}^{\perp})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{S^{2}}|\nabla_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}|^{2}-\int_{S^{2}}(\tilde{\psi}^{\perp})^{2}.

We recall that the eigenvalues of
-\Delta
on
S^{2}
are given by

\{k(k+1)\ |\ k\geq 0\}
.
The eigenvalue 0 has a constant eigenfunction and the eigenvalue
2
has eigenspace spanned by the coordinate functions
\pi_{i}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=x_{i}
, for
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})\in S^{2}
and
i=1,2,3
.
Let
(\lambda_{j})_{j\geq 0}
denote all eigenvalues, repeated according to multiplicity, with
\lambda_{0}=0
,
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=\lambda_{3}=2
, and let
(e_{j})_{j\geq 0}
denote the corresponding eigenfunctions so that they form an orthonormal system in
L^{2}(S^{2})
, and
e_{1},e_{2},e_{3}
are multiples of the coordinate functions
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\pi_{3}
.
We decompose
\tilde{\psi}
and
\tilde{g}
:

(9.16)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{j}e_{j},\quad\tilde{g}^{\perp}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j}e_{j},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{j}=\langle\tilde{\psi}^{\perp},e_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}(S^{2})},\quad\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j}=\langle\tilde{g}^{\perp},e_{j}\rangle_{L^{2}(S^{2})}.

Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\lambda_{j}-2)(\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}(\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}-(\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j=4}^{\infty}(\lambda_{j}-2)(\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}.

Equation (
9.15
) gives us that

(9.17)

\displaystyle(\lambda_{j}-2)\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{j}=2\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j},

and then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}+\sum_{j=4}^{\infty}\frac{2}{\lambda_{j}-2}(\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}.

By Lemma
9.2

\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{1}=\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{2}=\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{3}=0
. Therefore

(9.18)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=4}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{j}-2}(\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}

and

\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U=\sum_{j=4}^{\infty}(\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}.

This proves (
9.14
).

∎

Lemma 9.4
.

There exist positive constants
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
such that if

\phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
is radially symmetric and satisfies
(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}\phi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
with
0<\sigma<1
, and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi(y)dy=0,\quad

then

(9.19)

\displaystyle c_{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}\,\leq\,\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{-1}(\phi^{\perp})^{2}\,\leq\,c_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2},

(9.20)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(\psi^{\perp})^{2}\,\leq\,c_{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}.

Proof.

Using the same notation as in the proof of Lemma
9.3
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{-1}(\phi^{\perp})^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U[(\psi^{\perp})^{2}+2\psi^{\perp}g^{\perp}+(g^{\perp})^{2}]

\displaystyle=\int_{S^{2}}[(\tilde{\psi}^{\perp})^{2}+2\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}\tilde{g}^{\perp}+(\tilde{g}^{\perp})^{2}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}[(\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}+2\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{j}\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j}+(\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}].

As in the previous proof,
\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j}=0
for
j=0,1,2,3
. Using (
9.17
) we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{-1}(\phi^{\perp})^{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{3}(\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}+\sum_{j=4}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j}^{2}}{(\lambda_{j}-2)^{2}}(\tilde{g}^{\perp}_{j})^{2}.

This formula already gives

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U^{-1}(\phi^{\perp})^{2}.

We observe that
\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{1}=\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{2}=0
by radial symmetry.
We also have
\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{0}=0
, by (
9.17
).
Let

\displaystyle\hat{\psi}=\sum_{j=4}^{\infty}\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}_{j}e_{j}

and note that it satisfies

\displaystyle-\Delta_{S^{2}}\hat{\psi}-2\hat{\psi}=2\tilde{g}^{\perp}\quad\text{in }S^{2}.

By (
9.17
),

\displaystyle\|\hat{\psi}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}\leq C\|\tilde{g}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})},

and from elliptic estimates

(9.21)

\displaystyle\|\hat{\psi}\|_{C^{\alpha}(S^{2})}\leq C\|\tilde{g}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})},

for any
0<\alpha<1
.
Since
(1+|y|)^{3+\sigma}\phi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

and
\phi
has total mass 0, we have
(1+|y|)^{1+\sigma}\psi\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

(here the functions are radial) and also

(1+|y|)^{1+\sigma}\psi^{\perp}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
.
It follows that
\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}(P)
= 0 where
P=(0,0,1)
. Since
\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}
and
\hat{\psi}
differ by a constant times
\pi_{3}
we have

\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}=\hat{\psi}-\frac{\hat{\psi}(P)}{\pi_{3}(P)}\pi_{3},

where
\pi_{3}(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=x_{3}
.
This implies, by (
9.21
),

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\psi}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\hat{\psi}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}+C|\hat{\psi}(P)|\leq C\|\tilde{g}^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(S^{2})}.

This proves the other inequality in (
9.19
) and (
9.20
).

∎

Lemma 9.5
.

Suppose that
\phi=\phi(y,t)
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,
t>0
is a function satisfying

|\phi(y,t)|\leq\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{2+\sigma}},

with
0<\sigma<1
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi(y,t)\,dy

\displaystyle=0,\quad\forall t>0,

and that
\phi
is differentiable with respect to
t
and
\phi_{t}
satisfies also

\displaystyle|\phi_{t}(y,t)|\leq\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{2+\sigma}}.

Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi_{t}g=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g

where for each
t
,
g(y,t)
is defined as

\displaystyle g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta^{-1})\phi+c(t)

and
c(t)\in\mathbb{R}
is chosen so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g(y,t)U(y)\,dy=0.

Proof.

Using the notation of the previous lemma, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi_{t}g

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g_{t}+\psi_{t})g=2\int_{S^{2}}(\tilde{g}_{t}\tilde{g}+\tilde{\psi}_{t}\tilde{g}).

We have

\displaystyle-\Delta_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}-2\tilde{\psi}=2\tilde{g},\quad\text{in }S^{2}.

And differentiating in
t
we get

(9.22)

\displaystyle-\Delta_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}_{t}-2\tilde{\psi}_{t}=2\tilde{g}_{t},\quad\text{in }S^{2}.

Multiplying by
\tilde{g}
and integrating we find that

\displaystyle\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}_{t}\tilde{g}=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{S^{2}}\Delta\tilde{\psi}_{t}\tilde{g}-\int_{S^{2}}\tilde{g}_{t}\tilde{g}.

Thus

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi_{t}g=-\int_{S^{2}}\Delta\tilde{\psi}_{t}\tilde{g}

Decompose as in (
9.16
) and find that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi_{t}g=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}\lambda_{j}(\tilde{\psi}_{j})_{t}\tilde{g}_{j}

But from (
9.22
)

\displaystyle(\lambda_{j}-2)(\tilde{\psi}_{j})_{t}=2(\tilde{g}_{j})_{t}.

We note that
\tilde{g}_{j}=0
for
j=0,1,2,3
.
Indeed, this is true for
j=0
by the assumption

\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}gU=0
.
By Lemma
9.2
this is true also for
j=1,2,3
.
Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi_{t}g=\sum_{j=4}^{\infty}\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{j}-2}(\tilde{g}_{j})_{t}\tilde{g}_{j}

and the desired conclusion follows from (
9.18
).
∎

9.3.
A Poincaré inequality

Lemma 9.6
.

Let
B_{R}(0)\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be the open ball centered at 0 of radius
R
.
There exists
C>0
such that, for any
R>0
large and any
g\in H^{1}(B_{R})
with
\int_{B_{R}}g\,U\,dx=0
we have

\frac{C}{R^{2}}\int_{B_{R}}g^{2}U\leq\int_{B_{R}}|\nabla g|^{2}U.

Proof.

Using a Fourier decomposition we only need to consider the radial case, that is, we claim that
if
g(r)
satisfies

(9.23)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}g(r)\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr=0,

then there is
C
such that for all
R
large

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr\leq CR^{2}\int_{0}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

Let
0<\delta<1
to be fixed later on.
From (
9.23
) we have

\displaystyle\int_{\delta}^{R}g(r)\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr=-\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

But

\displaystyle\int_{\delta}^{R}g(r)\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\delta}^{R}g(r)\frac{d}{dr}\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}\Bigr)dr

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{g(R)}{1+R^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{g(\delta)}{1+\delta^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\delta}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}dr.

Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{|g(\delta)|}{1+\delta^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{|g(R)|}{1+R^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\delta}^{R}|g^{\prime}(r)|\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}dr+\int_{0}^{\delta}|g(r)|\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality

\displaystyle\int_{\delta}^{R}|g^{\prime}(r)|\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}dr\leq\Bigl(\int_{\delta}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr\Bigr)^{1/2}(\log R-\log\delta)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\delta}|g(r)|\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr\leq\delta\Bigl(\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr\Bigr)^{1/2}.

Hence

(9.24)

\displaystyle g(\delta)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{g(R)^{2}}{R^{4}}+2(\log R-\log\delta)\int_{\delta}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr+4\delta^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

We compute now

\displaystyle\int_{\delta}^{R}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\delta}^{R}g(r)^{2}\frac{d}{dr}\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}\Bigr)dr

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\frac{g(R)^{2}}{1+R^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\frac{g(\delta)^{2}}{1+\delta^{2}}+\int_{\delta}^{R}g(r)g^{\prime}(r)\frac{1}{1+r^{2}}dr.

Using (
9.24
) and the Cauchy-Schwartz inequality we get

\displaystyle\int_{\delta}^{R}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{1}{2}\frac{g(R)^{2}}{1+R^{2}}+\frac{g(R)^{2}}{R^{4}}+(\log R-\log\delta)\int_{\delta}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle\quad+2\delta^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr+AR^{2}\int_{\delta}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{AR^{2}}\int_{\delta}^{R}g(r)^{2}\frac{1}{r}dr.

But
\frac{1}{AR^{2}r}\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}
for
r\in[\delta,R]
if
A=4(1+\frac{1}{\delta^{2}})
and
R\geq 1
. Choosing
A=4(1+\frac{1}{\delta^{2}})
and
R\geq 2
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\delta}^{R}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle\leq[2AR^{2}+2(\log R-\log\delta)]\int_{\delta}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

(9.25)

\displaystyle\quad+4\delta^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

With
\delta>0
still to be chosen we get from (
9.24
) for
0<x<\delta

\displaystyle g(x)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2\frac{g(R)^{2}}{R^{4}}+2(\log R-\log x)\int_{0}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr+4x^{2}\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

Integrating we get

(9.26)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{2}\frac{g(R)^{2}}{R^{4}}+2\log R\int_{0}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr+\delta^{4}\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

Using the condition (
9.23
) we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}g(r)\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{R}g(r)\frac{d}{dr}\Bigl(\frac{r^{2}}{1+r^{2}}\Bigr)dr

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}g(R)\frac{R^{2}}{1+R^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)\frac{r^{2}}{1+r^{2}}dr.

Then

\displaystyle g(R)^{2}\leq 4R^{4}\int_{0}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

Using this combined with (
9.26
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle\leq\delta^{2}4\int_{0}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr+2\log R\int_{0}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle\quad+\delta^{4}\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

Taking
\delta=\frac{1}{2}
(this fixes
A
) gives

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\delta}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr

\displaystyle\leq 4(\log R+1)\int_{0}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

Combining this with (
9.25
) we get

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{R}g(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr\leq CR^{2}\int_{0}^{R}g^{\prime}(r)^{2}\frac{r}{(1+r^{2})^{2}}dr.

∎

10.
Linear theory: a decomposition

Here we consider

(10.1)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}\phi&=L[\phi]+B[\phi]+h,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},\infty),\\
\phi(\cdot,\tau_{0})&=\phi_{0}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

The results of this section are going to be used later only in the case of radial functions, so we make this assumption here.
We write in the rest of this section

\phi=\phi(y,\tau)=\phi(\rho,\tau)
, where
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
,

\rho=|y|
.

The operator
B
is assumed to be one of the following two:

(10.2)

\displaystyle B[\phi]=\zeta(\tau)(2\phi+y\cdot\nabla\phi)=\zeta(\tau)\nabla\cdot(y\phi),

or

(10.3)

\displaystyle B[\phi]=\zeta(\tau)y\cdot\nabla\phi,

where

\displaystyle\zeta(\tau)=-\frac{\zeta_{0}}{\tau\log\tau}+O\Big(\frac{1}{\tau(\log\tau)^{1+\sigma_{0}}}\Bigr),\quad\text{as }\tau\to\infty,

for some constants
\zeta_{0}>0
,
0<\sigma_{0}<1
.

We assume that
\|h\|_{**}<\infty
where

\displaystyle\|h\|_{**}=\inf K,\quad\text{such that }

\displaystyle|h(y,\tau)|\leq K\frac{1}{\tau^{\nu}(\log\tau)^{m}}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{6+\sigma}}\min\Bigl(1,\frac{\tau^{\epsilon/2}}{|y|^{\epsilon}}\Bigr),\quad\tau>\tau_{0},\ y\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

where
\nu>1
,
\epsilon>0
,
\sigma>0
,
m\in\mathbb{R}
.
This is the same norm as in (
8.6
).

We also assume that
h
has zero mass

(10.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}h(y,\tau)dy=0\quad\text{for all }\tau>\tau_{0},

and the same for the initial condition

(10.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi_{0}dy=0.

It follows from the equation (
10.1
), (
10.4
), and (
10.5
) that the solution
\phi
to (
10.1
) defined in §
8
satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi(y,\tau)dy=0\quad\text{for all }\tau>\tau_{0}.

We recall the decomposition of
\phi
introduced in §
9.2
. Given
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
with sufficient decay and mass zero,
we let
g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta^{-1})\phi
,
and define
a
so that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g+a)Udy=0
.
Then define
g^{\perp}=g+a
,
\psi^{\perp}=\psi-a(1+\frac{1}{2}z_{0})
, and

(10.6)

\displaystyle\phi^{\perp}=\phi-\frac{a}{2}Z_{0}.

Actually
a
is directly computed by

(10.7)

\displaystyle a=-\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Ug=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Gamma_{0}\phi.

In the time dependent situation
a=a(\tau)
and all functions depend on
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
and
\tau
.

A difficulty to obtain estimates is the presence of a kernel in the linear operator if
B=0
, since
Z_{0}
satisfies
L[Z_{0}]=0
.
It can be proved that the solution
\phi
of (
10.1
) with zero initial condition and
\|h\|_{**}<\infty
has the bound

\displaystyle\sup_{y}|\phi(y,\tau)|\leq C\Bigl(\frac{\log\tau_{0}}{\log\tau}\Bigr)^{2\zeta_{0}-\sigma_{0}}\|h\|_{**},

and probably this estimate cannot be improved much. Also
\phi
has a some decay at spatial infinity and in particular it has finite second moment

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\phi(y,\tau)|\,|y|^{2}\,dy<\infty,\quad\tau>\tau_{0}.

Therefore
Z_{0}
doesn’t describe well the class of solution we want to consider, even for the case
B=0
, in which
\zeta(\tau)\equiv 0
.

A better candidate to describe the solutions
\phi
of (
10.1
) with zero initial condition and
\|h\|_{**}<\infty
is obtained by considering the initial value problem

(10.8)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}Z_{B}&=L[Z_{B}]+B[Z_{B}],\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},\infty),\\
Z_{B}(\cdot,\tau_{0})&=\tilde{Z}_{0}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}.\end{aligned}\right.

where
\tilde{Z}_{0}
is defined as

(10.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{0}(\rho)

\displaystyle=(Z_{0}(\rho)-m_{Z_{0}}U)\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{\tau_{0}}}\Bigr),

and
m_{Z_{0}}
is selected so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{Z}_{0}dy=0.

Note that since
Z_{0}
has mass zero and decays like
1/\rho^{4}
we have

m_{Z_{0}}=O(\frac{1}{\tau_{0}})
.

We will then consider the problem

(10.10)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}\phi&=L[\phi]+B[\phi]+h,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},\infty),\\
\phi(\cdot,\tau_{0})&=c_{1}\tilde{Z}_{0}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\end{aligned}\right.

for radial functions
\phi
,
h
,
\phi_{0}
, where
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
is a parameter.
We assume that
\|h\|_{**}<\infty
.

Proposition 10.1
.

Let us assume that
1<\nu<\frac{7}{4}
.
Then there is
C>0
such that for any
\tau_{0}
sufficiently large the following holds. Suppose that
\|h\|_{**}<\infty
is radially symmetric and satisfies the zero mass condition (
10.4
).
Then there exists
c_{1}
such that the solution
\phi=\phi^{\perp}+\frac{a}{2}Z_{0}
of (
10.10
) satisfies

\displaystyle|a(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{f(\tau)R(\tau)^{2}}{(\log\tau_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{**},

(10.11)

\displaystyle|\phi^{\perp}(\rho,\tau)|\leq Cf(\tau)R(\tau)\frac{1}{1+|y|^{2}}\|h\|_{**}.

where
R(\tau)>0
is defined by

(10.12)

\displaystyle R(\tau)^{2}=\frac{\tau}{(\log\tau)^{q}},

where
0<q<1
,
and

(10.13)

\displaystyle f(\tau)=\frac{1}{\tau^{\nu}(\log\tau)^{m}}.

Moreover
c_{1}
is a linear function of
h
and satisfies

\displaystyle|c_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}{(\log\tau_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{**}.

We always decompose
\phi
as in (
10.6
):

\displaystyle\phi=\phi^{\perp}+\frac{a(\tau)}{2}Z_{0}

and write

\displaystyle g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi,\quad g^{\perp}=\frac{\phi^{\perp}}{U}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi^{\perp}.

Let us denote

(10.14)

\displaystyle\omega(\tau)=\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R(\tau)}(0)}Ug(\tau)^{2}\Bigr)^{1/2}.

The strategy for the proof of Proposition
10.1
is contained in the following lemmas.
The first one is an a-priori estimate for the solution, assuming that
a(T_{2})=0
for some
T_{2}
.

Lemma 10.1
.

There is
C
such that for
\tau_{0}
large the following holds.
Suppose that
\|h\|_{**}<\infty
is radially symmetric and satisfies the zero mass condition (
10.4
) and
consider (
10.10
).
Let
\phi^{\perp}
,
a
be the decomposition (
10.6
).
Suppose that for some
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
there is
T_{2}>\tau_{0}
is such that

\displaystyle a(T_{2})=0.

Then

(10.15)

\displaystyle|a(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{f(\tau)R(\tau)^{2}}{(\log\tau_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{**},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},T_{2}]

(10.16)

\displaystyle|\omega(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{f(\tau)R(\tau)}{(\log\tau_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{**},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},T_{2}]

(10.17)

\displaystyle|c_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}{(\log\tau_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{**}.

The constant
C
is independent of
T_{2}
and
c_{1}
.

There is a variant of the previous lemma, where the hypothesis
a(T_{2})=0
is replaced by an assumption about its time decay.

Lemma 10.2
.

There is
C
such that for
\tau_{0}
large the following holds.
Suppose that
\|h\|_{**}<\infty
is radially symmetric and satisfies the zero mass condition (
10.4
) and
consider (
10.10
).
Let
\phi^{\perp}
,
a
be the decomposition (
10.6
).
Suppose that for some
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\frac{a}{fR^{2}}\in L^{\infty}(\tau_{0},\infty).

Then

(10.18)

\displaystyle|a(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{f(\tau)R(\tau)^{2}}{(\log\tau_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{**},\quad\tau>\tau_{0}

(10.19)

\displaystyle|\omega(\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{f(\tau)R(\tau)}{(\log\tau_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{**},\quad\tau>\tau_{0},

(10.20)

\displaystyle|c_{1}|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}{(\log\tau_{0})^{1-q}}\|h\|_{**}.

Lemma 10.3
.

Let
Z_{B}
be the solution to (
10.8
) and write it as
Z_{B}=Z_{B}^{\perp}+\frac{a_{Z}}{2}Z_{0}
according to the decomposition (
10.6
). Then
a_{Z}(\tau)\not=0
for all
\tau\geq\tau_{0}
.

Lemma 10.4
.

There is
C
such that for
\tau_{0}
large the following holds.
Suppose that
\|h\|_{**}<\infty
is radially symmetric and satisfies the zero mass condition (
10.4
).
Then there is a unique
c_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
the solution
\phi=\phi^{\perp}+\frac{a}{2}Z_{0}
of (
10.10
) (as in (
10.6
)) satisfies (
10.18
), (
10.19
) and (
10.20
).

In the first results we do some computations and obtain some estimates, which are used as technical steps in the main argument.

The next lemma is a calculation to help us deal with the term
B
when we multiply the equation by a suitable test function. It holds for operators more general than
B
as in (
10.2
) and (
10.3
). Let

\displaystyle\tilde{B}[\phi]=\zeta_{1}(\tau)\phi+\zeta_{2}(\tau)y\cdot\nabla\phi,

with
\zeta_{1}(\tau)
,
\zeta_{2}(\tau)
satisfying

(10.21)

\displaystyle|\zeta_{i}(\tau)|\leq\frac{C}{\tau\log\tau}\quad\text{for all }\tau>\tau_{0}.

Lemma 10.5
.

We have

(10.22)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{B}[\phi]g^{\perp}\right|\leq\frac{C}{\tau\log\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}dy+C\frac{|a(\tau)|}{\tau\log\tau}\|\nabla g^{\perp}U^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\tilde{B}[\phi]g^{\perp}dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}[\zeta_{1}(\tau)\phi+\zeta_{2}(\tau)y\cdot\nabla\phi]g^{\perp}dy.

By Lemma
9.3
and the hypothesis (
10.21
) we have

(10.23)

\displaystyle\left|\zeta_{1}(\tau)\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}dy\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\tau\log\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}dy.

Let us write

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla\phi(y)g^{\perp}(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla\phi^{\perp}(y)g^{\perp}(y)dy+\frac{a(\tau)}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla Z_{0}(y)g^{\perp}(y)dy.

We claim that

(10.24)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla\phi^{\perp}(y)g^{\perp}(y)dy\right|\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}Udy.

Indeed, we write

(10.25)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla\phi^{\perp}(y)g^{\perp}(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla(Ug^{\perp})g^{\perp}(y)dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla(U\psi^{\perp})g^{\perp}(y)dy.

But

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla(Ug^{\perp})g^{\perp}(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla U(g^{\perp})^{2}(y)dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Uy\cdot\nabla g^{\perp}g^{\perp}(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla U(g^{\perp})^{2}(y)dy+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Uy\cdot\nabla[(g^{\perp})^{2}](y)dy

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla U(g^{\perp})^{2}(y)dy-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}(y)dy,

and so

(10.26)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla(Ug^{\perp})g^{\perp}(y)dy\right|\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}Udy.

The second term in (
10.25
) is:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla(U\psi^{\perp})g^{\perp}(y)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(y\cdot\nabla U)\psi^{\perp}g^{\perp}(y)dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(y\cdot\nabla\psi^{\perp})g^{\perp}(y)dy.

We estimate the first term above

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(y\cdot\nabla U)\psi^{\perp}g^{\perp}(y)dy\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\psi^{\perp})^{2}Udy\Bigr)^{1/2}\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}Udy\Bigr)^{1/2}

(10.27)

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}Udy,

by (
9.20
).
To estimate
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(y\cdot\nabla\psi^{\perp})g^{\perp}(y)dy
we write it using radial symmetry:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(y\cdot\nabla\psi^{\perp})g^{\perp}(y)dy=2\pi\int_{0}^{\infty}U(\rho)(\psi^{\perp})^{\prime}(\rho)g^{\perp}(\rho)\rho^{2}d\rho.

We use that

\psi^{\perp}
satisfies

-\Delta\psi^{\perp}-U\psi^{\perp}=Ug^{\perp}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\psi^{\perp}(\rho,\tau)\to 0\quad\text{as }\rho\to\infty.

Then, by the variations of parameters formula, since that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Ug^{\perp}z_{0}dy=0
, we have

\displaystyle(\psi^{\perp})^{\prime}(\rho)=z_{0}^{\prime}(\rho)\int_{\rho}^{\infty}U(r)g^{\perp}(r)\bar{z}_{0}(r)r\,dr+\bar{z}_{0}^{\prime}(\rho)\int_{0}^{\rho}U(r)g^{\perp}(r)z_{0}(r)r\,dr,

where
\bar{z}_{0}
is a second linear independent function in the kernel of
\Delta+U
satisfying

\displaystyle|\bar{z}_{0}(\rho)|\leq C(|\log\rho|+1).

We then compute

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}U(\rho)(\psi^{\perp})^{\prime}(\rho)g^{\perp}(\rho)\rho^{2}d\rho=I_{1}+I_{2}

where

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\rho}^{\infty}U(\rho)U(r)z_{0}^{\prime}(\rho)\bar{z}_{0}(r)g^{\perp}(r)g^{\perp}(\rho)\rho^{2}rdrd\rho

\displaystyle I_{2}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{\rho}^{\infty}U(\rho)U(r)\bar{z}_{0}^{\prime}(\rho)z_{0}(r)g^{\perp}(r)g^{\perp}(\rho)\rho^{2}rdrd\rho.

We directly check that

\displaystyle|I_{1}|+|I_{2}|\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}Udy.

From this we get that

(10.28)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U(y\cdot\nabla\psi^{\perp})g^{\perp}(y)dy\right|\leq C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}Udy.

Combining (
10.25
), (
10.26
), (
10.27
), (
10.28
) we obtain (
10.24
).

Next we claim that

(10.29)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla Z_{0}(y)g^{\perp}(y)dy\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\|\nabla g^{\perp}U^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}.

Indeed, write

\displaystyle y\cdot\nabla Z_{0}

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(yZ_{0})-2Z_{0}=\nabla\cdot(yZ_{0}-2\nabla z_{0})-4Z_{0}

where
z_{0}
is defined in (
9.2
) and satisfies the linearized Liouville equation
\Delta z_{0}+Uz_{0}=0
. We have used here that
Z_{0}=Uz_{0}
.
So

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla Z_{0}(y)g^{\perp}(y)dy=-\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(yZ_{0}-2\nabla z_{0})\nabla g^{\perp}dy-4\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g^{\perp}Z_{0}dy.

But
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Z_{0}g^{\perp}dy=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Uz_{0}g^{\perp}dy=0
by Lemma
9.2
, and
|yZ_{0}-2\nabla z_{0}|\leq\frac{C}{|y|^{4}}
, so

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla Z_{0}(y)g^{\perp}(y)dy\right|

\displaystyle\leq C\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{4}}|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}dy\Bigr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\|\nabla g^{\perp}U^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}.

This proves (
10.29
).

From (
10.23
), (
10.24
) and (
10.29
) we conclude the validity of (
10.22
).

∎

In the next lemma we get an estimate for
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}
, but with right hand side that depends on the solution.

Lemma 10.6
.

We make the same assumptions of Proposition
10.1
.
Let
f
be given by (
10.13
),

\omega
be defined in (
10.14
)
and let
R:[\tau_{0},\infty)\to(0,\infty)
be continuous.
There is
c>0
,
\varepsilon>0
and
C>0
such that for
\tau_{0}
sufficiently large, if

(10.30)

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\geq\tau_{0}}\frac{R^{2}(\tau)}{\tau\log\tau}\leq\varepsilon

then

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}+\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}\|h\|_{**}^{2}+C\frac{a(\tau)^{2}}{R^{4}}+C\frac{\omega(\tau)^{2}}{R^{2}},

for some constant
c>0
.

Proof.

Equation (
10.10
) can be written in the form

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\phi=\nabla\cdot(U\nabla g^{\perp})+B[\phi]+h,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\times(\tau_{0},\infty).

We multiply this equation by
g^{\perp}
and integrate on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, using Lemma
9.5
:

(10.31)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}B[\phi]g^{\perp}+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}hg^{\perp}.

Let
H=(-\Delta)^{-1}h
, and observe that, since
h
is radial and
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}hdy=0
,

\displaystyle|\nabla H(\rho,\tau)|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{\rho}\int_{\rho}^{\infty}h(s,\tau)sds\right|\leq Cf(\tau)\|h\|_{**}\frac{1}{(1+\rho)^{5+\sigma}}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}hg^{\perp}\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla\cdot\nabla Hg^{\perp}\right|=\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\nabla H\cdot\nabla g^{\perp}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}+C\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla H|^{2}U^{-1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}+Cf(\tau)^{2}\|h\|_{**}^{2}.

This combined with (
10.31
) gives

(10.32)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}\leq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}B[\phi]g^{\perp}\right|+Cf(\tau)^{2}\|h\|_{**}^{2}.

We use the inequality in Lemma
9.6
to get

(10.33)

\displaystyle\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{B_{R}}(g^{\perp}-\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R})^{2}U\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2},

for some
c>0
,
where

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R}=\frac{1}{\int_{B_{R}}U}\int_{B_{R}}g^{\perp}U.

From

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U=\int_{B_{R}}(g^{\perp}-\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R})^{2}U+2\int_{B_{R}}g^{\perp}\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R}U-\int_{B_{R}}(\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R})^{2}U

we get

\displaystyle\int_{B_{R}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U\leq 2\int_{B_{R}}(g^{\perp}-\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R})^{2}U+C(\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R})^{2}.

so, using (
10.33
),

\displaystyle\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{B_{R}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}+C\frac{1}{R^{2}}(\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R})^{2},

for a new
c>0
.
This implies

\displaystyle\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}+C\frac{1}{R^{2}}(\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R})^{2}+C\frac{1}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R}}U(g^{\perp})^{2}.

Using that
g^{\perp}=g+a
we get

(10.34)

\displaystyle\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}+C\frac{1}{R^{2}}(\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R})^{2}+C\frac{\omega^{2}}{R^{2}}+C\frac{a^{2}}{R^{4}}.

But

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g^{\perp}Udy=0

and this implies

\displaystyle\bar{g}^{\perp}_{R}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\int_{B_{R}}U}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R}}g^{\perp}U

so

\displaystyle(\bar{g}_{R}^{\perp})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U\leq\frac{Ca^{2}}{R^{4}}+\frac{C}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R}}g^{2}U.

This combined with (
10.34
) gives

\displaystyle\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}+C\frac{a^{2}}{R^{4}}+C\frac{\omega^{2}}{R^{2}}.

We use this together with (
10.32
) to obtain (for a new
c>0
)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\partial_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}

\displaystyle+\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U+\frac{1}{4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}U|\nabla g^{\perp}|^{2}

(10.35)

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}B[\phi]g^{\perp}\right|+Cf(\tau)^{2}\|h\|_{**}^{2}+C\frac{a^{2}}{R^{4}}+C\frac{\omega^{2}}{R^{2}}.

We obtain from Lemma
10.5
and the assumption (
10.30
) that

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}B[\phi]g^{\perp}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\tau\log\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}Udy+C\frac{|a(\tau)|}{\tau\log\tau}\|\nabla g^{\perp}U^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{\tau\log\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}Udy+\frac{|a(\tau)|^{2}}{R^{4}}+C\frac{R^{4}}{\tau^{2}(\log\tau)^{2}}\|\nabla g^{\perp}U^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(10.36)

\displaystyle=C\frac{\varepsilon}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}Udy+\frac{|a(\tau)|^{2}}{R^{4}}+C\varepsilon^{2}\|\nabla g^{\perp}U^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Taking
\varepsilon>0
small, and combining (
10.35
) and (
10.36
) we get

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}+\frac{1}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}\|h\|_{**}^{2}+\frac{Ca^{2}}{R^{4}}+C\frac{\omega^{2}}{R^{2}}.

By Lemma
9.3
we obtain

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}+\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}\|h\|_{**}^{2}+C\frac{a^{2}}{R^{4}}+C\frac{\omega^{2}}{R^{2}},

for some constant
c>0
,
which is the desired conclusion.
∎

The next lemma provides a pointwise estimate for
g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta^{-1})\phi

assuming a certain bound for
\|U^{1/2}g\|_{L^{2}}
.

Lemma 10.7
.

Assume
\nu>0
. Let
\phi
be the solution to (
10.10
) as in §
8
. Suppose that
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{0}
and

(10.37)

\displaystyle\|g(\tau)U^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq K_{1}f_{1}(\tau),\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}],

where
K_{1}\geq 0
and

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)=\frac{(\log\tau)^{\mu}}{\tau^{\nu-1}},

where
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then

\displaystyle|U(y)g(y,\tau)|\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}+\frac{|c_{1}|}{f_{1}(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{2}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

Proof.

We define

g_{0}=Ug,

and obtain from (
10.1
) the equation

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}g_{0}=U\partial_{\tau}g

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}\phi-U(-\Delta^{-1})\partial_{\tau}\phi

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot\Bigl[U\nabla\Bigl(\frac{g_{0}}{U}\Bigr)\Bigr]-U(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\nabla\cdot(U\nabla g)\right]+h-U(-\Delta)^{-1}h

(10.38)

\displaystyle\quad+B[g_{0}]+B[U\psi[g_{0}]]-U(-\Delta)^{-1}(B[g_{0}+U\psi[g_{0}]]),

where we regard
\psi[g_{0}]
as the operator that maps
g_{0}
to the unique radial solution to

(10.39)

\displaystyle-\Delta\psi-U\psi=g_{0}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\psi(\rho,\tau)\to 0\quad\text{as }\rho\to\infty.

We note that this problem has indeed a solution since
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g_{0}z_{0}dy=0
by Lemma
9.2
, which is unique by imposing
\psi(\rho,\tau)\to 0
as
\rho\to\infty
in the radial setting. This solution is given by the variations of parameters formula

\displaystyle\psi(\rho,\tau)=z_{0}(\rho)\int_{\rho}^{\infty}g_{0}(r,\tau)\bar{z}_{0}(r)r\,dr+\bar{z}_{0}(\rho)\int_{0}^{\rho}g_{0}(r,\tau)z_{0}(r)r\,dr,

where
\bar{z}_{0}
is a second linear independent function in the kernel of
\Delta+U
satisfying
|\bar{z}_{0}(\rho)|\leq C(|\log\rho|+1)
.

We compute

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot(U\nabla g)

\displaystyle=\Delta gU+\nabla U\cdot\nabla g=\Delta(gU)-\nabla U\cdot\nabla g-g\Delta U,

and hence

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{-1}[\nabla\cdot(U\nabla g)]

\displaystyle=-gU-(-\Delta)^{-1}\left[\nabla U\cdot\nabla g+g\Delta U\right]

\displaystyle=-gU-v

where

(10.40)

\displaystyle v:=(-\Delta)^{-1}(\nabla\cdot(g_{0}\nabla\Gamma_{0})).

We write (
10.38
) as

(10.41)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}g_{0}

\displaystyle=\Delta g_{0}-\nabla g_{0}\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}+2Ug_{0}+B[g_{0}]+\tilde{h}

where

(10.42)

\displaystyle\tilde{h}=Uv+B[U\psi[g_{0}]]-U(-\Delta)^{-1}(B[g_{0}+U\psi[g_{0}]])+h-U(-\Delta)^{-1}h.

Note that since we are working with radial functions, we can integrate (
10.40
) explicitly and obtain

(10.43)

\displaystyle v(\rho,\tau)=\int_{\rho}^{\infty}g_{0}(s,\tau)\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(s)ds.

We claim that for any
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
:

(10.44)

\displaystyle\|\tilde{h}\|_{L^{p}(B_{1}(y))}\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{4-\frac{4}{p}}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

Indeed, let us start with

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}|v(\rho)|^{p}U(\rho)\rho d\rho

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\Bigl(\int_{\rho}^{\infty}U(s)g(s)^{2}sds\Bigr)^{p/2}\Bigl(\int_{\rho}^{\infty}U(s)\frac{\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}(s)^{2}}{s}ds\Bigr)^{p/2}U(\rho)\rho d\rho

(10.45)

\displaystyle\leq C\|gU^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}^{p},

which follows from (
10.43
) and Hölder’s inequality

Let us write
\psi=\psi[g_{0}]
and
\tilde{\psi}=\psi\circ\Pi
, where
\Pi
is the stereographic projection.
Writing (
10.39
) in
S^{2}
and using standard
L^{p}
theory we find that for any
p>2

\|\tilde{\psi}\|_{L^{\infty}(S^{2})}+\|\nabla_{S^{2}}\tilde{\psi}\|_{L^{p}(S^{2})}\leq C\|gU^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})},

which implies

(10.46)

\displaystyle\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}+\left(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|\nabla\psi|^{p}U^{1-\frac{p}{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C\|gU^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
From (
10.37
)
we see that

\displaystyle\|g_{0}(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{2}(B_{1}(y))}\leq CK_{1}f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{2}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}],

and from (
10.37
) and (
10.45
) we have

(10.47)

\displaystyle\|Uv(\cdot,\tau)\|_{L^{p}(B_{1}(y))}\leq CK_{1}f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{4-\frac{4}{p}}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

Similarly, inequalities (
10.46
) and (
10.37
) imply

(10.48)

\displaystyle\|B[U\psi[g_{0}]]\|_{L^{p}(B_{1}(y))}\leq CK_{1}f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{\tau\log\tau}\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{4}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

Let’s estimate

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{-1}(B[g_{0}+U\psi[g_{0}]])=\zeta_{1}(\tau)(-\Delta)^{-1}(y\cdot\nabla(g_{0}+U\psi[g_{0}]))+\zeta_{2}(\tau)(-\Delta)^{-1}(g_{0}+U\psi[g_{0}]).

Note that
\psi=(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi=(-\Delta)^{-1}(g_{0}+U\psi)
.
But we can estimate
\psi
from

(10.49)

\displaystyle\psi(\rho)=z_{0}(\rho)\int_{\rho}^{\infty}\frac{1}{z_{0}(r)^{2}r}\int_{r}^{\infty}g_{0}(s)z_{0}(s)sds,\quad\rho>1.

Then (
10.37
) yields

(10.50)

\displaystyle|\psi(\rho,\tau)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{1+\rho}\|gU^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq CK_{1}f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{1+\rho},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}],

and so

(10.51)

\displaystyle|U(-\Delta)^{-1}(g_{0}+U\psi[g_{0}])|\leq CK_{1}f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{1+|y|^{5}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

Concerning the term
(-\Delta)^{-1}(y\cdot\nabla(g_{0}+U\psi))
,
we notice that if we let
w=g_{0}+U\psi
, then
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}y\cdot\nabla w=0
, and

\displaystyle(-\Delta)^{-1}(y\cdot\nabla w)(\rho)=\int_{\rho}^{\infty}rw(r,\tau)dr-2\psi(\rho,\tau).

Using (
10.37
) and (
10.50
) we get

\displaystyle\left|((-\Delta)^{-1}(y\cdot\nabla(g_{0}+U\psi)))(\rho,\tau)\right|\leq CK_{1}f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{1+\rho},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

From this and (
10.51
) we find that

(10.52)

\displaystyle|U(-\Delta)^{-1}(B[g_{0}+U\psi[g_{0}]])(y,\tau)|\leq CK_{1}f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{\tau\log\tau(1+|y|)^{5}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

Finally the estimates

(10.53)

\displaystyle\|h\|_{L^{p}(B_{1}(y))}+\|U(-\Delta)^{-1}h\|_{L^{p}(B_{1}(y))}\leq C\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{4-\frac{4}{p}}}

are directly obtained.

Combining (
10.47
), (
10.48
), (
10.52
), and (
10.53
) we deduce (
10.44
).

From equation (
10.41
), the estimate (
10.44
), standard parabolic
L^{p}
estimates restricted to
B_{1}(y)\times(\max(\tau-1,\tau_{0}),\tau)
and embedding into Hölder spaces, we deduce that

(10.54)

\displaystyle|g_{0}(y,\tau)|\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}+\frac{|c_{1}|}{f_{1}(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{2}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

This is the desired conclusion.
We also get from (
10.54
):

(10.55)

\displaystyle|v(y,\tau)|\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}+\frac{|c_{1}|}{f_{1}(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{2}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

∎

In some of the proofs below the following barrier will be useful.
Consider the equation

(10.56)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \partial_{\tau}\phi&=\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}\phi+h\quad\text{in }(\tau_{0},\infty)\times\mathbb{R}^{6}\\
\phi(\tau_{0},\cdot)&=0\end{aligned}

where
\Delta_{\mathbb{R}^{6}}
is the laplacian in
\mathbb{R}^{6}
.
Suppose that
h
has the estimate

|h(y,\tau)|\leq\frac{1}{\tau^{\gamma+1}}\frac{1}{(1+|y|/\sqrt{\tau})^{b}}

for some
\gamma,b\in\mathbb{R}
.

If
\gamma<3
and
\gamma<\frac{b}{2}
then there is a barrier satisfying

C_{1}\frac{1}{\tau^{\gamma}}\frac{1}{(1+|y|/\sqrt{\tau})^{b}}\leq\phi(y,\tau)\leq C_{2}\frac{1}{\tau^{\gamma}}\frac{1}{(1+|y|/\sqrt{\tau})^{b}}.

Indeed, we can consider all functions to be radial and write
\rho=|y|
,
y\in\mathbb{R}^{6}
.
Let

(10.57)

\displaystyle\bar{\phi}(\rho,\tau)=\frac{1}{\tau^{\gamma}}g\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{\tau}}\Bigr),\quad\zeta=\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{\tau}}.

Then

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\bar{\phi}-\Bigl(\partial_{\rho\rho}+\frac{5}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}\Bigr)\bar{\phi}=-\frac{1}{\tau^{\gamma+1}}\Bigl[g^{\prime\prime}(\zeta)+\frac{5}{\zeta}g^{\prime}(\zeta)+\frac{\zeta}{2}g^{\prime}(\zeta)+\gamma g(\zeta)\Bigr].

Let
g_{1}(\zeta)=\frac{1}{(1+\zeta^{2})^{b/2}}
.
Since
\gamma<\frac{b}{2}
we have

-\Bigl[g_{1}^{\prime\prime}(\zeta)+\frac{5}{\zeta}g_{1}^{\prime}(\zeta)+\frac{\zeta}{2}g_{1}^{\prime}(\zeta)+\gamma g_{1}(\zeta)\Bigr]\geq\frac{c}{\zeta^{b}},\quad\zeta\geq M,

for some
c,M>0
.
Let
g_{0}(\zeta)=e^{-\frac{\zeta^{2}}{4}}
be the Gaussian kernel, which satisfies

g_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\zeta)+\frac{5}{\zeta}g_{0}^{\prime}(\zeta)+\frac{\zeta}{2}g_{0}^{\prime}(\zeta)+3g_{0}(\zeta)=0.

Let
g=C_{1}g_{0}+g_{1}
. Since
\gamma<3
, we can find
C_{1}
large so that

-\Bigl[g^{\prime\prime}(\zeta)+\frac{5}{\zeta}g^{\prime}(\zeta)+\frac{\zeta}{2}g^{\prime}(\zeta)+\gamma g(\zeta)\Bigr]\geq\frac{c}{1+\zeta^{b}},\quad\zeta>0.

Then
\bar{\phi}
defined by (
10.57
) with
g=C_{1}g_{0}+g_{1}
is a supersolution to (
10.56
).

In the next lemma we improve the spatial decay of
g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta^{-1})\phi
.

Lemma 10.8
.

Assume
1<\nu<\frac{7}{4}
. Let
\phi
be the solution to (
10.10
) as in §
8
. Suppose that
\tau_{1}\geq\tau_{0}
and

\displaystyle\|g(\tau)U^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})}\leq K_{1}f_{1}(\tau),\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}],

where
K_{1}\geq 0
and

\displaystyle f_{1}(\tau)=\frac{(\log\tau)^{\mu}}{\tau^{\nu-1}},

where
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
.
Then

(10.58)

\displaystyle|U(\rho)g(\rho,\tau)|\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}+\frac{|c_{1}|}{f_{1}(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+\rho)^{4}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

Proof.

We us the same notation as in Lemma
10.7
and consider (
10.41
) for
g_{0}=Ug
with
\tilde{h}
defined in (
10.42
).
We are going to use barriers to estimate
g_{0}
.

We claim that
\tilde{h}
satisfies

(10.59)

\displaystyle|\tilde{h}(y,\tau)|\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\Bigl(\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{6}}+\frac{1}{\tau\log\tau(1+|y|)^{5}}\Bigr),\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

Indeed, from (
10.54
) and (
10.55
) we find that

(10.60)

\displaystyle|-Uv+h-U(-\Delta)^{-1}h|\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+|y|)^{6}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

To estimate
B[U\psi[g_{0}]]
we use (
10.50
) a similar estimate for
\partial_{\rho}\psi
, and the assumptions on
\zeta_{1}
,
\zeta_{2}
in (
10.21
), to obtain

\displaystyle|B[U\psi[g_{0}]|\leq CK_{1}f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{\tau\log\tau}\frac{1}{1+|y|^{5}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

This, (
10.60
) and (
10.52
) prove (
10.59
).

To get better spatial decay we construct a barrier and apply the maximum principle to equation (
10.41
) in
(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R_{0}}(0))\times(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
, where
R_{0}
is a fixed large constant.
Several of constants
C
below depend on
R_{0}
but we will not keep track of the explicit dependence.

The linear operator for
g_{0}
in (
10.41
), acting on radial functions with
\rho=|y|
, is given by:

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}g_{0}-[\Delta g_{0}-\nabla g_{0}\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}+B[g_{0}]+2Ug_{0}]

\displaystyle=\partial_{\tau}g_{0}-\partial_{\rho\rho}g_{0}-\frac{1}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}g_{0}-\frac{4\rho}{1+\rho^{2}}\partial_{\rho}g_{0}+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{1+\rho^{4}}\Bigr)g_{0}

\displaystyle\quad+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{\tau\log\tau}\Bigr)g_{0}+O\Bigl(\frac{1}{\tau\log\tau}\Bigr)\rho\partial_{\rho}g_{0}.

The main part outside of a ball
B_{R_{0}}(0)
with
R_{0}
big is given by
\partial_{\tau}-\partial_{\rho\rho}-\frac{5}{\rho}\partial_{\rho}
.

By (
10.59
) we need to construct
\bar{g}_{1}
such that

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\bar{g}_{1}-[\Delta\bar{g}_{1}-\nabla\bar{g}_{1}\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}+B[\bar{g}_{1}]+2U\bar{g}_{1}]\geq h_{1}

where

\displaystyle h_{1}(\rho,\tau)

\displaystyle=f_{1}(\tau)\Bigl(\frac{1}{(1+\rho)^{6}}+\frac{1}{\tau\log\tau(1+\rho)^{5}}\Bigr).

To construct
\bar{g}_{1}
, let
0<\vartheta<1
, and let
\tilde{g}_{1}(\rho)
be radial and solve

-\Delta_{6}\tilde{g}_{1}=\frac{1}{1+\rho^{6-\vartheta}}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{6},

such that
\tilde{g}_{1}(\rho)(1+\rho^{4-\vartheta})
is bounded below and above by positive constants.
Let

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{1}(\rho,\tau)

\displaystyle=f_{1}(\tau)\tilde{g}_{1}(\rho)\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{\delta\sqrt{\tau}}\Bigr)+C_{1}\frac{f_{1}(\tau)}{\tau^{2-\vartheta/2}(1+\rho/\sqrt{\tau})^{5}}+C_{2}\frac{f_{1}(\tau)}{\tau^{2-\vartheta/2}}e^{-\frac{\rho^{2}}{4\tau}},

For appropriate
\delta>0
,
C_{1}
, and
C_{2}
, the function
\bar{g}_{1}(\rho,\tau)
is a supersolution in
(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R_{0}}(0))\times(\tau_{0},\tau_{1})
for the right hand side
h_{1}
. More precisely, writing
M=R_{0}^{2-\vartheta}(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}+\frac{1}{f_{1}(\tau_{0})}|c_{1}|)
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{\tau}-[\Delta-\nabla(\cdot)\cdot\nabla\Gamma_{0}+B]\right)M\bar{g}_{1}\geq|\tilde{h}|,\quad\text{in }(\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R_{0}}(0))\times(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

\displaystyle M\bar{g}_{1}\geq|g_{0}|,\quad\text{on }\rho=R_{0},\ \tau\in(\tau_{0},\tau_{1}),

because of Lemma
10.7
, and

\displaystyle M\bar{g}_{1}(\tau_{0})\geq\left|c_{1}Ug_{\tilde{Z}_{0}}\right|,\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},

where

\displaystyle g_{\tilde{Z}_{0}}=\frac{\tilde{Z}_{0}}{U}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\tilde{Z}_{0},

is the function
g
associated to
\tilde{Z}_{0}
defined in (
10.9
).
We note that
|Ug_{\tilde{Z}_{0}}(\rho)|\leq C\frac{1}{1+\rho^{4}}
and is supported on
\rho\leq 2\sqrt{\tau_{0}}
.
Here we are using that
\nu<\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\vartheta}{2}
.

Using the maximum principle we get

\displaystyle|g_{0}(y,\tau)|\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}+\frac{|c_{1}|}{f_{1}(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+\rho)^{4-\vartheta}},\quad\tau\in[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}].

The constant
C
here depends on
R_{0}
, but
R_{0}
is fixed and we will not keep track of the dependence of
C
on
R_{0}
.

By (
10.43
) and (
10.49
) we have

\displaystyle|\tilde{h}(y,\tau)|\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}+\frac{|c_{1}|}{f_{1}(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\Bigl(\frac{1}{(1+\rho)^{6+\sigma}}+\frac{1}{\tau\log\tau(1+\rho)^{6-\vartheta}}\Bigr).

We can now repeat the argument with a new barrier.
Consider
\tilde{g}_{2}(\rho)
the radial solution to

(10.61)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &-\Delta_{6}\tilde{g}_{2}=\frac{1}{1+\rho^{6+\sigma}}\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{6},\quad c_{1}\frac{1}{1+\rho^{4}}\leq\tilde{g}_{2}(\rho)\leq c_{2}\frac{1}{1+\rho^{4}},\end{aligned}

where
c_{1}
,
c_{2}>0
.
Let

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{2}(\rho,\tau)

\displaystyle=f_{1}(\tau)\tilde{g}_{2}(\rho)\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{\delta\sqrt{\tau}}\Bigr)+C_{1}\frac{f_{1}(\tau)}{\tau^{2}(1+\rho/\sqrt{\tau})^{6-\vartheta}}+C_{2}\frac{f_{1}(\tau)}{\tau^{2}}e^{-\frac{\rho^{2}}{4\tau}}.

For appropriate constants
\delta
,
C_{1}
,
C_{2}
, and assuming that
\nu<2-\frac{\vartheta}{2}
we get a suitable supersolution and we obtain

\displaystyle|g_{0}(y,\tau)|\leq C\Bigl(K_{1}+\frac{\|h\|_{**}}{R(\tau_{0})}+\frac{|c_{1}|}{f_{1}(\tau_{0})}\Bigr)f_{1}(\tau)\frac{1}{(1+\rho)^{4}}.

This proves (
10.58
).

The restriction on
\nu
were
\nu<\frac{3}{2}+\frac{\vartheta}{2}
and
\nu<2-\frac{\vartheta}{2}
. Choosing
\vartheta=\frac{1}{4}
we find that for
\nu<\frac{7}{4}
both barriers work.
∎

The next result is a technical step used in several places.

Lemma 10.9
.

Let
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{2}\to\mathbb{R}
be radial such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi=0
and
|\phi(y)|\leq\frac{C}{(1+|y|)^{2+\sigma}}
for some
\sigma>0
. Let
g=\frac{\phi}{U}-(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi
and assume that
\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}<\infty
. Then

(10.62)

\displaystyle|\phi(y)|\leq C\frac{\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}}{(1+|y|)^{4}}.

Proof.

Let
\psi=(-\Delta)^{-1}\phi
.
Since
\psi
satisfies

-\Delta\psi-U\psi=Ug\quad\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\psi(\rho)\to 0\quad\text{as }\rho\to\infty,

we have necessarily

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}Ugz_{0}dy=0.

We have the variations of parameters formula

(10.63)

\displaystyle\psi(\rho)=z_{0}(\rho)\int_{\rho}^{\infty}\frac{1}{z_{0}(r)^{2}r}\int_{r}^{\infty}Ug(s,\tau)z_{0}(s)s\,ds\,dr,\quad\rho>1.

From (
10.63
) we find

\displaystyle|\psi(\rho,\tau)|\leq C\|g\|_{L^{\infty}}.

This and the formula
\phi=Ug+U\psi
gives (
10.62
).
∎

Next we give a proof of Proof of Lemma
10.1
, but first we point some estimates of
\tilde{Z}_{0}
defined in (
10.9
). Using the general decomposition (
10.6
), we write

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{0}=\tilde{Z}_{0}^{\perp}+\frac{\tilde{a}}{2}Z_{0}.

By (
10.7
)

\displaystyle\tilde{a}=\frac{1}{8\pi}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\Gamma_{0}\tilde{Z}_{0}=2+O(\frac{\log\tau_{0}}{\tau_{0}}).

Hence
\tilde{Z}_{0}^{\perp}
satisfies

\displaystyle\tilde{Z}_{0}^{\perp}(\rho)

\displaystyle=\tilde{Z}_{0}(\rho)-\frac{a(\tau_{0})}{2}Z_{0}(\rho)

\displaystyle=(Z_{0}(\rho)-m_{Z_{0}}U(\rho))\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{\rho}{\sqrt{\tau_{0}}}\Bigr)-\Bigl(1+O(\frac{\log\tau_{0}}{\tau_{0}})\Bigr)Z_{0}(\rho)

(10.64)

\displaystyle=O\Bigl(\frac{\log\tau_{0}}{\tau_{0}}\frac{1}{1+\rho^{4}}\Bigr).

Proof of Lemma
10.1
.

We let
R
be defined by (
10.12
).
We multiply equation (
10.10
) by
g^{\perp}
and integrate in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Using Lemmas
10.6
and
9.3
we get

(10.65)

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}+\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}\|h\|_{**}^{2}+\frac{Ca^{2}}{R^{4}}+\frac{C}{R^{2}}\omega(\tau)^{2},

for some
c>0
, where

\displaystyle\omega(\tau)=\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R(\tau)}}g^{2}U\Bigr)^{1/2}.

Let us write

\displaystyle\|\varphi\|_{\infty,T_{2}}=\|\varphi\|_{L^{\infty}(\tau_{0},T_{2})},

and note that

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}^{2}<\infty,\quad\Bigl\|\frac{\omega}{Rf}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}<\infty.

The following inequalities are valid for
\tau_{0}<\tau<T_{2}
.
From (
10.65
) we get

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}+\frac{c}{R^{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi g^{\perp}\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}\Bigl(\|h\|_{**}^{2}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}^{2}+\Bigl\|\frac{\omega}{Rf}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}^{2}\Bigr).

By Gronwall’s inequality and Lemma
9.3
we get

(10.66)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U

\displaystyle\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}R(\tau)^{2}\Bigl(\|h\|_{**}^{2}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}^{2}+\Bigl\|\frac{\omega}{Rf}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}^{2}+c_{1}^{2}D(\tau_{0})^{2}\Bigr)

where

\displaystyle D(\tau_{0})=\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})}\frac{\log\tau_{0}}{\tau_{0}},

and we have used (
10.64
).

From (
10.66
) we find

(10.67)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g^{2}U\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}R(\tau)^{4}\Bigl(\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\|h\|_{**}^{2}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigl\|\frac{\omega}{Rf}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}^{2}+c_{1}^{2}\frac{D(\tau_{0})^{2}}{R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigr)

Using Lemma
10.8
we get

(10.68)

\displaystyle|Ug|\leq Cf(\tau)R(\tau)^{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\|h\|_{**}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\Bigl\|\frac{\omega}{Rf}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+|c_{1}|\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigr)\frac{1}{(1+\rho)^{4}},

where we have used that for
\tau_{0}
large,
\frac{D(\tau_{0})}{R(\tau_{0})}<\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}
.

We use this to estimate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus B_{R}}g^{2}U\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}R(\tau)^{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\|h\|_{**}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\Bigl\|\frac{\omega}{Rf}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+|c_{1}|\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigr)^{2},

which implies

\displaystyle\frac{\omega(\tau)}{R(\tau)f(\tau)}\leq C\Bigl(\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\|h\|_{**}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\Bigl\|\frac{\omega}{Rf}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+|c_{1}|\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigr).

We deduce that

(10.69)

\displaystyle\Bigl\|\frac{\omega}{Rf}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}\leq C\Bigl(\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\|h\|_{**}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+|c_{1}|\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigr).

Combining this inequality with (
10.67
) we obtain

(10.70)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}g^{2}U\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}R(\tau)^{4}\Bigl(\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\|h\|_{**}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+|c_{1}|\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigr)^{2},

and with (
10.66
) we get

(10.71)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp})^{2}U\leq Cf(\tau)^{2}R(\tau)^{2}\Bigl(\|h\|_{**}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+|c_{1}|\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigr)^{2}.

Going back to (
10.68
) we find

(10.72)

\displaystyle|Ug(\rho,\tau)|\leq Cf(\tau)R(\tau)^{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\|h\|_{**}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty}+|c_{1}|\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigr)\frac{1}{1+\rho^{4}}.

Using Lemma
10.9
we also obtain

(10.73)

\displaystyle|\phi(\rho,\tau)|\leq Cf(\tau)R(\tau)^{2}\Bigl(\frac{1}{R(\tau_{0})}\|h\|_{**}+\Bigl\|\frac{a}{R^{2}f}\Bigr\|_{\infty,T_{2}}+|c_{1}|\frac{1}{f(\tau_{0})R(\tau_{0})^{2}}\Bigr)\frac{1}{1+\rho^{4}}.

We multiply the equation satisfied by
\phi
(
10.10
) by
|y|^{2}\chi_{0}(\frac{y}{R})
, and integrate on
\mathbb{R}^{2}

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi|y|^{2}\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{y}{R}\Bigr)dy

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(L[\phi]+h)|y|^{2}\chi_{0}(\frac{y}{R})dy+\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}B[\phi]|y|^{2}\chi_{0}(\frac{y}{R})dy

(10.74)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{R^{\prime}(\tau)}{R}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi|y|^{2}\nabla\chi_{0}(\frac{y}{R})\cdot\frac{y}{R}dy,

where
R^{\prime}=\frac{dR}{d\tau}
.
Now integrate from
\tau
to
T_{2}
and use the decomposition (
10.6
).

We integrate (
10.74
) from
\tau
to
T_{2}
, use the decomposition (
10.6
) and that
a(T_{2})=0
to get

\displaystyle|a(\tau)|\log\tau

\displaystyle\leq\left|\int_{\tau}^{T_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(L[\phi(s)]+h)|y|^{2}\chi_{0}(\frac{y}{R(s)})dyds\right|+\left|\int_{\tau}^{T_{2}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}B[\phi(s)]|y|^{2}\chi_{0}(\frac{y}{R(s)})dyds\right|

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\int_{\tau}^{T_{2}}\frac{R^{\prime}(s)}{R(s)}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi(s)|y|^{2}\nabla\chi_{0}(\frac{y}{R(s)})\cdot\frac{y}{R(s)}dyds\right|

(10.75)

\displaystyle\quad+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{\perp}(T_{2})|y|^{2}\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{y}{R(T_{2})}\Bigr)dy\right|+\left|\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\phi^{\perp}(\tau)|y|^{2}\chi_{0}\Bigl(\frac{y}{R(\tau)}\Bigr)dy\right|.

By Lemma
9.4
and (
10.71
)

\displaystyle\int_{B_{2R(\tau)}}|\phi(\tau)^{\perp}||y|^{2}dy

\displaystyle\leq CR(\tau)\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(\phi^{\perp}(\tau))^{2}U^{-1}\Bigr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq CR(\tau)\Bigl(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}(g^{\perp}(\tau))^{2}U\Bigr)^{1/2}

(10.76)
